\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{J}\bar{K}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}g^{\bar{I}L}\bigg(\frac{\partial g_{L\bar{J}}}{\partial\bar{\phi}^{\bar{K}}}+\frac{\partial g_{L\bar{K}}}{\partial\bar{\phi}^{\bar{J}}}\bigg),\quad\tilde{T}^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{J}\bar{K}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}g^{\bar{I}L}\bigg(\frac{\partial g_{L\bar{J}}}{\partial\bar{\phi}^{\bar{K}}}-\frac{\partial g_{L\bar{K}}}{\partial\bar{\phi}^{\bar{J}}}\bigg),

the
symmetric connection
and the
torsion
associated with
g_{I\bar{J}}
. Then
\mathscr{L}_{1}
is defined by

(3.1.8)

\mathscr{L}_{1}\coloneqq\bar{Q}\bigg(g_{I\bar{J}}\chi_{\mu}^{I}(\partial_{\mu}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{J}})\bigg)=g_{I\bar{J}}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{I}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{J}}+g_{I\bar{J}}\chi^{I}_{\mu}\tilde{\nabla}_{\mu}\eta^{\bar{J}},

where
\tilde{\nabla}_{\mu}
is a covariant derivative with respect to the connection
\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{J}\bar{K}}+\tilde{T}^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{J}\bar{K}}
, i.e. we have

(3.1.9)

\tilde{\nabla}_{\mu}\eta^{\bar{I}}=\partial_{\mu}\eta^{\bar{I}}+(\partial_{\mu}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{J}})(\tilde{\Gamma}^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{J}\bar{K}}+\tilde{T}^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{J}\bar{K}})\eta^{\bar{K}}.

Moreover, if
g_{I\bar{J}}
is a Kähler metric, then
\tilde{T}^{\bar{I}}_{\bar{J}\bar{K}}=0
.

3.2.
Perturbative quantization

The partition function of the RW model is

(3.2.1)

Z_{M}(\Sigma_{3})\coloneqq\int e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S}\mathscr{D}[\phi^{i}]\mathscr{D}[\eta^{I}]\mathscr{D}[\chi^{I}_{\mu}],

where
S\coloneqq\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\mathscr{L}
and
\mathscr{D}
is a formal measure.

As mentioned in
[
RW97
]
(see also
[
Tho99
,
HT99
]
), in order to do a perturbative expansion around critical points of the action (which are constant maps from
\Sigma_{3}
to
M
), we need to deal with the
zero modes
:

•

bosonic zero modes
: they are constant modes of
\phi
;

•

fermionic zero modes
: here we should distinguish two cases

–

if
M
is a rational homology sphere (i.e. the first Betti number
b_{1}=0
), the fermionic zero modes are the constant modes of
\eta
. There are
2n
zero modes if
\dim M=4n
.

–

if
M
is not a rational homology sphere but the first Betti number
b_{1}>0
, then there are
2nb_{1}
zero modes of
\chi_{\mu}
additionally.

Taking into account the zero modes, one can decompose
\phi^{i}=\phi^{i}_{0}+\phi^{i}_{\perp}
, where
\phi^{i}_{0}
are the constant maps and
\phi^{i}_{\perp}
are required to be orthogonal to
\phi^{i}_{0}
. Similarly, the
\eta^{I}
are also decomposed as
\eta^{I}=\eta^{I}_{0}+\eta^{I}_{\perp}
, where
\eta^{I}_{0}
are harmonic 0-forms with coefficients in the fiber
V_{\phi_{0}}
of the
\Sp(n)
-bundle
V\rightarrow X
and
\eta^{I}_{\perp}
are orthogonal to the harmonic part.

For our purposes, we will only consider the Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}_{2}
, in light of these decompositions we can rewrite it as

(3.2.2)

\mathscr{L}_{2}=\frac{1}{2}\bigg(\omega_{IJ}(\phi_{0})\chi^{I}d\chi^{J}+\frac{1}{3}R_{IJK\bar{L}}(\phi_{0})\chi^{I}\chi^{J}\chi^{K}\eta^{\bar{L}}_{0}+\frac{1}{3}(\nabla\Omega_{IK})(\partial\phi^{I}_{\perp})\chi^{K}\chi^{L}\bigg).

As a result of an analysis on the absorption of fermionic zero modes by the Feynman diagrams, Rozansky and Witten concluded that only diagrams with trivalent vertices contribute. Moreover, these trivalent vertices have to be exactly
2n
to saturate the
2n
zero modes of
\eta
. They call these diagrams “minimal”. The Lagrangian
L_{2}
contains the following vertex with the needed properties

(3.2.3)

V=\frac{1}{6}R_{IJK\bar{L}}(\phi_{0})\chi^{I}\chi^{J}\chi^{K}\eta^{\bar{L}}_{0}.

Here we should think of
\eta_{0}
as a “coupling constant”, in fact, we should focus on the order of the
\eta^{I}_{0}
during the perturbative expansion.

Since all the fields
\eta
are used to absorb zero modes, we only need the propagators for the fields
\phi^{i}
and
\chi^{I}_{mu}
. According to
[
RW97
]
, these are

(3.2.4)

\begin{split}&\braket{\chi^{I}_{\mu}(x_{1}),\chi^{J}_{\nu}(x_{2})}=\hbar\omega^{IJ}G^{\chi}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2}),\\
&\braket{\phi^{i}(x_{1}),\phi^{j}(x_{2})}=-\hbar g^{ij}G^{\phi}(x_{1},x_{2}),\end{split}

with
G^{\chi}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})
and
G^{\phi}(x_{1},x_{2})
Green’s functions. We refer to
[
RW97
]
for a detailed description of the Green’s functions.

The Feynman diagrams participating in the calculation of the partition function depend only on the dimension of the target manifold
M
and on the first Betti number
b_{1}
of the source 3-manifold
\Sigma_{3}
. The former causes the number of vertices of the graphs to be equal to
2n
. The latter has consequences on the valence of the vertices. We have the following cases:

•

(
b_{1}=0
) There are no
\chi
zero modes to absorb. Hence, all the Feynman diagrams are closed graphs with
2n
trivalent vertices. This is the case when
\Sigma_{3}
is a rational homotopy sphere.

•

(
b_{1}=1
) There are
2n

\chi
zero modes coming from a harmonic 1-form. As a consequence, each vertex absorbs exactly one zero mode of
\chi
, and thus all the Feynman diagrams are closed graphs with
2n
bivalent vertices.

•

(
b_{1}=2
) There are
4n

\chi
zero modes coming from a two harmonic 1-forms on
\Sigma_{3}
. As a consequence, each vertex absorbs exactly two zero modes of
\chi
, one for each harmonic 1-form, and thus all the Feynman diagrams are closed graphs with
2n
univalent vertices.

•

(
b_{1}=3
) There are
6n

\chi
zero modes coming from three harmonic 1-forms on
\Sigma_{3}
. As a consequence, each vertex absorbs exactly three zero modes of
\chi
, one for each harmonic 1-form, and thus all the Feynman diagrams are a collection of
2n
totally disconnected vertices with no edges.

•

(
b_{1}\geq 4
) The
\chi
zero modes become too many and they can not be integrated out by the
\chi
present in the vertices (at most three), so the RW partition function vanishes.

Let us denote by
\Gamma_{n,m}
the set of all closed graphs with
2n

m
-valent vertices and as
Z_{M,\Gamma}(\Sigma_{3};\phi^{i}_{0})
the sum of all the contributions of the minimal Feynman diagrams corresponding to a given graph
\Gamma
. The total contribution of Feynman diagrams is

(3.2.5)

Z_{M}(\Sigma_{3};\phi^{i}_{0})=\sum_{\Gamma\in\Gamma_{n,3-b_{1}(\Sigma_{3})}}Z_{M,\Gamma}(\Sigma_{3};\phi^{i}_{0}),

where each
Z_{M,\Gamma}(\Sigma_{3};\phi^{i}_{0})
can actually be written as a sum of two factors

(3.2.6)

Z_{M,\Gamma}(\Sigma_{3};\phi^{i}_{0})=\text{W}_{\Gamma}(M;\phi_{0}^{i})\sum_{a}I_{\Gamma,a}(\Sigma_{3}).

We will explain each factor one-by-one. First,
I_{\Gamma,a}(\Sigma_{3})
includes the integral over
\Sigma_{3}
of the propagators
G^{\chi}_{\mu\nu}(x_{1},x_{2})
and
G^{\phi}(x_{1},x_{2})
as well as
\chi
zero modes. The sum is over all the possible ways to contract the fields of
\Gamma
with the propagators (
3.2.4
). On the other hand, the factor
\text{W}_{\Gamma}(M;\phi^{i}_{0})
is a product of tensors
R_{IJKL}
coming from the vertices
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, which are contracted by the
\omega^{IJ}
contained in the propagators. After antisymmetrizing over the anti-holomorphic indices (coming from the zero modes’ contributions), we obtain a
\bar{\partial}
-closed
(0,2n)
-form on
M
. In other words, we have a map

(3.2.7)

\Gamma_{n,3}\rightarrow H^{0,2n}(M),

where
H^{0,2n}(X)
is the Dolbeault cohomology. This corresponds to a weight system, the
Rozansky–Witten weight system
. By definition a function on
\Gamma_{n,3}
is called a weight if it satisfies the AS and IHX relations (see also
[
Bar95
]
).

The AS relation means that
\Gamma_{n,3}
is antisymmetric under the permutation of legs at a vertex. For RW, this is not valid on the nose since the curvature tensor is completely symmetric. However, we can prove the vanishing of tadpole diagrams (i.e. diagrams with a loop centered at a vertex), which is consistent with the AS relation. The proof follows simply because the loop is constructed by contracting two indices of the symmetric tensor
R_{IJK\bar{L}}\eta^{\bar{L}}_{0}
with
\omega^{IJ}
, which is antisymmetric.

On the other hand, the IHX relation means that the sum over all possible (three) ways of collapsing a propagator such that we obtain a graph with a four-valent vertex, while the other vertices are trivalent, vanishes (see Fig.
3.2.1
).

+

+

=0

Figure 3.2.1
.
The IHX relation. It holds whenever the graphs are identical outside the dashed circle.

Explicitly, the sum of the three contributions is equal to the expression

(3.2.8)

\eta^{\bar{L}}_{0}\eta^{\bar{L^{\prime}}}_{0}\omega^{IJ}\bigg(R_{IJK\bar{L}}R_{I^{\prime}J^{\prime}K^{\prime}\bar{L^{\prime}}}+R_{IJK^{\prime}\bar{L}}R_{I^{\prime}J^{\prime}K\bar{L^{\prime}}}+R_{IJJ^{\prime}\bar{L}}R_{I^{\prime}KK^{\prime}\bar{L^{\prime}}}\bigg)-\bar{L}\leftrightarrow\bar{L^{\prime}},

with the notation
\bar{L}\leftrightarrow\bar{L^{\prime}}
we mean that we are subtracting the same quantity with the indices
\bar{L}
and
\bar{L}^{\prime}
switched, in this way the expression vanishes. In other words, the IHX relation follows as a result of the Bianchi identity for the curvature tensor
R
. The validity of the IHX relation ensures we are obtaining topological invariants of 3-manifolds in the perturbative expansion of the partition function
[
Saw04
]
.

At this point, we can take the product of the
(0,2n)
-form which is the image of the graph
\Gamma
with the
(2n,0)
-form
\omega^{n}\in H^{2n,0}(M)
and integrate the resulting
(2n,2n)
-form over
M
. In this way we obtain the weights
b_{\Gamma}(M)
, which are numbers called
Rozansky–Witten invariants
studied by Sawon in
[
Saw04
]
. More explicitly, we have

(3.2.9)

b_{\Gamma}(M)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2n}}\int_{M}\text{W}_{\Gamma}(M;\phi^{i}_{0})\omega^{n}.

Finally, the RW partition function is shown to be
[
RW97
]

(3.2.10)

Z_{M}(\Sigma_{3})=\Big|H_{1}(\Sigma_{3},\mathbb{Z})\Big|^{\prime}\sum_{\Gamma\in\Gamma_{n,3-b_{1}(\Sigma_{3})}}b_{\Gamma}(M)\sum_{a}I_{\Gamma,a},

where
\Big|H_{1}(\Sigma_{3},\mathbb{Z})\Big|^{\prime}
is the number of torsion elements in
H_{1}(\Sigma_{3},\mathbb{Z})
(see
[
FG91
]
).

3.3.
Comparison with Chern–Simons theory

In this section, we are going to explore briefly the similarities between CS theory and the RW theory as exhibited in
[
RW97
]
. The main message is that RW is a kind of “Grassmann odd version” of CS theory. Let us make this more precise.

Recall the CS Lagrangian

(3.3.1)

\mathscr{L}_{\text{CS}}=\mathrm{Tr}\bigg(AdA+\frac{2}{3}A^{3}\bigg).

Let us compare it with the RW Lagrangian in (
3.2.2
). As we can see from Table
3.3.1
(where we denote by
T_{a}
the generators of the Lie algebra and
f_{abc}
the structure constants), there is almost a direct match. We are using the word “almost” because the symmetry properties of the various objects in the table are reversed:
\mathrm{Tr}T_{a}T_{b}
is symmetric in its arguments while the holomorphic symplectic form
\omega_{IJ}
is antisymmetric,
f_{abc}
is totally antisymmetric whereas
R_{IJKL}\eta^{L}_{0}
is totally symmetric. However, this should not come as a surprise since by definition
A^{a}
is an anti-commuting object, while
\chi^{I}
is commuting.

CS

RW

A^{a}

\chi^{I}

\mathrm{Tr}T_{a}T_{b}

\omega_{IJ}

f_{abc}

R_{IJKL}\eta^{L}_{0}

Table 3.3.1
.
Comparison between CS theory and RW theory.

By doing the associations in the table, the vertex in CS is the same as the vertex in RW. It follows that the diagrams of the two theories coincide. Consequently, the partition function differs only for the weight factors since for RW they are proportional to the curvature tensor of
M
rather than the structure constants of a Lie group.

Other similarities come at the level of gauge-fixing. We refer the interested reader to
[
RW97
,
HT99
]
for a detailed discussion.

Remark 3.3.1
.

There is an important difference between CS and RW theories. In the RW model, the vertex carries an odd Grassmann odd harmonic mode
\eta^{I}_{0}
, hence it can never appear more than
2n
times in any diagram. This corresponds to a natural
cut-off
of the perturbative expansion of the RW model.

4.
Classical formal globalization

The idea is to construct a 3-dimensional topological sigma model, which, when globalized, reduces to the original RW model. In particular, we are interested in the formulation of the RW model with target a holomorphic symplectic manifold
13
13

13

This construction reduces to the RW model considered in the bulk of
[
RW97
]
, when we consider as target a hyperKähler manifold.
(i.e. a complex symplectic manifold with a holomorphic symplectic form, see also the appendix in
[
RW97
]
). Hence, let
M
be a holomorphic symplectic manifold endowed with coordinates
X^{i}
and
X^{\bar{i}}
, and with a holomorphic symplectic form
\Omega=\Omega_{ij}\delta X^{i}\delta X^{j}
, i.e. a closed, non degenerate
(2,0)
form. Moreover, consider a 3-dimensional manifold
\Sigma_{3}
and construct an AKSZ sigma model
14
14

14

See Section
2.4
for an introduction.
with source
T[1]\Sigma_{3}
and target
M
. In this case, the space of maps is

(4.0.1)

\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}}\coloneqq\Maps\bigg(T[1]\Sigma_{3},M\bigg).

On the source manifold, we choose bosonic coordinates
\{u\}
(ghost degree 0) on
\Sigma_{3}
and fermionic odd coordinates
\{\theta\}
(ghost degree 1) on the fibers of
T[1]\Sigma_{3}
. Moreover, by picking up local coordinates
X^{i}
on
M
, maps in
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}}
can be described by a superfield
\mathbf{X}
, whose components are chosen as:

(4.0.2)

\mathbf{X}^{i}=X^{i}(u)+\theta^{\mu}X^{i}_{\mu}(u)+\frac{1}{2!}\theta^{\mu}\theta^{\nu}X^{i}_{\mu\nu}(u)+\frac{1}{3!}\theta^{\mu}\theta^{\nu}\theta^{\chi}X^{i}_{\mu\nu\chi}(u),

where
X^{i}
is a 0-form,
X^{i}_{\mu}
is a 1-form etc. To these maps maps
X^{i},X^{i}_{\mu},\dots
we assign ghost degrees such that the ghost degree of
\mathbf{X}
is equal to the one of
X^{i}
(that is 0), for example
X^{i}_{\mu\nu}
has form degree 2 and ghost degree
-2
.

Now, we can define a symplectic form for the space of fields. Since it should have ghost degree
-1
, we assign ghost degree
15
15

15

More precisely, we add a formal parameter
q
of ghost degree 2 in front of
\Omega_{ij}
. The parameter is immediately suppressed from the notation.
2 to
\Omega_{ij}
(in this way the target manifold has degree 2 and the AKSZ construction can be done without problems) and define

(4.0.3)

\omega_{\Sigma_{3}}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\delta\mathbf{X}^{i}\delta\mathbf{X}^{j}\bigg),

where by
\delta
we denote the de Rham differential on the space of fields. Since we have a canonical Berezinian
\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}
on
T[1]\Sigma_{3}
of degree
-3
, the symplectic form has degree
-1
as we desired. Hence, the space of fields is equipped with an odd Poisson bracket
(-,-)
.We have an associated AKSZ action given by

(4.0.4)

\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3}}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\mathbf{X}^{i}D\mathbf{X}^{j}\bigg),

where
D=\theta^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{\mu}}
is the differential on
T[1]\Sigma_{3}
. When
\Sigma_{3}
is a closed manifold, the action
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3}}
satisfies the CME

(4.0.5)

(\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3}},\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3}})=0.

Equivalently, we can introduce a cohomological Hamiltonian vector field
Q_{\Sigma_{3}}
on
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}}
defined by

(4.0.6)

\iota_{Q_{\Sigma_{3}}}\omega_{\Sigma_{3}}=\delta S_{\Sigma_{3}}.

This vector field has the following form

(4.0.7)

Q_{\Sigma_{3}}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(D\mathbf{X}^{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbf{X}^{i}}\bigg).

The above can be restated by saying that
(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}},\omega_{\Sigma_{3}},\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3}})
is a BV manifold.

In the presence of boundaries, the model can be extended to a BV-BFV theory by associating the BV-BFV manifold

(4.0.8)

(\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}},\omega^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}=\delta\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}},\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}},Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}})

over the BV manifold
(\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}},\omega_{\Sigma_{3}},Q_{\Sigma_{3}})
, with the following set of data

(4.0.9)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}=\Maps(T[1]\partial\Sigma_{3},X),\\
&\mathcal{S}^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}=\int_{T[1]\partial\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\mathbf{X}^{i}D\mathbf{X}^{j}\bigg),\\
&\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}=\int_{T[1]\partial\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\mathbf{X}^{i}\delta\mathbf{X}^{j}\bigg),\\
&Q^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}=\int_{T[1]\partial\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(D\mathbf{X}^{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\mathbf{X}^{i}}\bigg),\end{split}

with
\mu_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}
the Berezinian on the boundary
\partial\Sigma_{3}
of degree
-2
. The data is such that

(4.0.10)

\iota_{Q_{\Sigma_{3}}}\omega_{\Sigma_{3}}=\delta\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3}}+\pi^{*}\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}.

Remark 4.0.1
.

A possible modification of the model consists in coupling the target manifold with
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}[1]\otimes\mathfrak{g}[1]
or
\mathfrak{g}[1]
, forming thus the “
BF
-RW” model and the “CS-RW” model
[
KQZ13
]
, respectively. In this way, after globalization, one should get an extension of the results obtained by Källén, Qiu and Zabzine
[
KQZ13
]
.

4.1.
Globalization

In the last section, we introduced a very simple AKSZ sigma model. Here we globalize that construction using methods of formal geometry
[
GK71
,
Bot11
]
(see Appendix
B
for an introduction) following
[
CMW19
]
. First, we expand around critical points of the kinetic part of the action. The Euler–Lagrange equations for our model are simply
d\mathbf{X}^{i}=0
, which means that the component of
\mathbf{X}^{i}
of ghost degree 0 is a constant map: we denote it by
x^{i}
and we think of it as a
background field

[
Mos21
]
. Moreover, since we want to vary
x
itself, we lift the fields as the pullback of a formal exponential map at
x
. We also note that the fields
\mathbf{X}^{\bar{i}}
are just
spectators
, which means that they do not contribute to the action, hence we can think of taking constant maps also in the antiholomorphic direction.

The above allows to linearize the space of fields
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}}
by working in the formal neighbourhoods of the constant map
x\in M
. We define the following
holomorphic formal exponential map

(4.1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi:\ T^{1,0}M&\rightarrow M\\
(x,y)&\mapsto\varphi^{i}(x,y)=x^{i}+y^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi^{i}_{jk}(x^{i},x^{\bar{i}})y^{j}y^{k}+\dots\end{split}

Remark 4.1.1
.

We think about the holomorphic formal exponential map here defined as an extension to the complex case of the formal exponential map used in e.g.
[
CF01
]
. This notion should correspond to the “canonical coordinates” introduced in
[
Ber+94
]
and the holomorphic exponential map applied by Kapranov to the RW case in
[
Kap99
]
.

The formal exponential map lifts
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}}
to

(4.1.2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tilde{\varphi}_{x}:\ \tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}:=\Maps(T[1]\Sigma_{3},T^{1,0}_{x}M)&\rightarrow\Maps(T[1]\Sigma_{3},M)\\
\hat{\mathbf{X}}&\mapsto\mathbf{X}\end{split}

which is given by precomposition with
\varphi^{-1}_{x}
, i.e.
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\varphi^{-1}_{x}\circ\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}}
and
\mathbf{X}=\varphi_{x}(\hat{\mathbf{X}})
. Now, since the target is linear, we can write the space of fields as

(4.1.3)

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma_{3})\otimes T^{1,0}_{x}M.

Consequently, we lift the BV action, the BV 2-form and the primitive 1-form obtaining:

(4.1.4)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x}:=\mathrm{T}\tilde{\varphi}_{x}^{*}\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3}}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}\,\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}D\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j}\bigg),\\
&\omega_{\Sigma_{3},x}\coloneqq\tilde{\varphi}^{*}_{x}\omega_{\Sigma_{3}}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}\,\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j}\bigg),\\
&\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3},x}\coloneqq\tilde{\varphi}^{*}_{x}\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}=\int_{T[1]\partial\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}\,\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j}\bigg),\end{split}

where
\mathrm{T}
denotes the Taylor expansion around the fiber coordinates
\{y\}
at zero.
This set of data satisfies the mCME for any
x\in M
:

(4.1.5)

\iota_{Q_{\Sigma_{3},x}}\omega_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\delta\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x}+\pi^{*}\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3},x},

with
Q_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}\,\Big(D\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\frac{\delta}{\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}}\Big)
. Hence, we have a BV-BFV manifold associated to the space of fields
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}
.

The next remark introduces an important ingredient to write down the globalized action.

Remark 4.1.2
.

The constant map
x:T[1]\Sigma_{3}\rightarrow M
in
\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}}
can be thought of as an element in
M
. Hence, we have a natural inclusion
M\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}}
. We exploit this fact by defining, for a constant field
x
and
\mathbf{X}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Sigma_{3}}
, a 1-form:

(4.1.6)

R_{\Sigma_{3}}=\Big(R_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}(x;\mathbf{X})dx^{j}+\Big(R_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x;\mathbf{X})dx^{\bar{j}}\in\Omega^{1}\Big(M,\Der\Big(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee 1,0}M)\Big)\Big).

As before, we lift this 1-form to
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}
. This lift, denoted by
\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}
, is locally written as:

(4.1.7)

\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}=\Big(\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})dx^{j}+\Big(\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x;\mathbf{X})dx^{\bar{j}}.

4.2.
Variation of the classical background

So far, the classical background
x
has been fixed. However, our aim is to vary
x
and construct a global formulation of the action. Hence, we understand the collection
\{\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x}\}_{x\in M}
as a map
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}}
to be given by
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}}:x\mapsto S_{\Sigma_{3},x}
and we compute how it changes over
M
. In order to accomplish this task, inspired by
[
CMW19
,
CMW20
,
KQZ13
,
BCM12
]
, choosing a background field
x\in M
, we define

(4.2.1)

\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R}\coloneqq\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{j}+\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)=\mathcal{S}_{R}+\mathcal{S}_{\bar{R}}.

The integrand is a well defined term of degree 3, since we assigned degree 2 to the symplectic form and
\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}
is a 1-form on
M
. After integration,
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R}
is then of total degree 0.

The term
\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}
has been introduced in Remark
4.1.2
. However, its connection with the globalization procedure is not clear. To explain it, we introduce the
classical Grothendieck connection
adapted to our case (see Appendix
[
CF01
]
).

Definition 4.2.1

(Classical Grothendieck connection)
.

Given a holomorphic formal exponential map
\varphi
, we can define the associated
classical Grothendieck connection
on
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee 1,0}M)
, given by
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}\coloneqq d_{M}+R
, where
d_{M}
is the sum of the holomorphic and antiholomorphic Dolbeault differentials on
M
and
R\in\Omega^{1}\Big(M,\Der\Big(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee 1,0}M)\Big)\Big)
. By using local coordinates
\{x\}
on the basis and
\{y\}
on the fibers, we have
R=R_{j}(x;y)dx^{j}+R_{\bar{j}}(x;y)dx^{\bar{j}}
, where
R_{j}=R^{i}_{j}(x;y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
and
R_{\bar{j}}=R^{i}_{\bar{j}}(x;y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
with

(4.2.2)

\begin{split}&R^{i}_{j}(x;y)dx^{j}:=-\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y}\bigg)^{-1}\bigg]^{i}_{p}\frac{\partial\varphi^{p}}{\partial x^{j}}dx^{j},\\
&R^{i}_{\bar{j}}(x;y)dx^{\bar{j}}:=-\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y}\bigg)^{-1}\bigg]^{i}_{p}\frac{\partial\varphi^{p}}{\partial x^{\bar{j}}}dx^{\bar{j}}.\end{split}

Note that
R^{i}_{j}(x;y)
and
R^{i}_{\bar{j}}(x;y)
are formal power series in the second argument, namely

(4.2.3)

\begin{split}R^{i}_{j}(x;y)&=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}R^{i}_{j;j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}(x)y^{j_{1}}\dots y^{j_{k}},\\
R^{i}_{\bar{j}}(x;y)&=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}R^{i}_{{\bar{j}};j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}(x)y^{j_{1}}\dots y^{j_{k}}.\end{split}

Remark 4.2.2
.

The classical Grothendieck connection has a couple of important properties:

•

It is flat, which can be rephrased by saying that the following equation is satisfied

(4.2.4)

d_{M}R+\frac{1}{2}[R,R]=0.

•

A section
\sigma
is closed under
\mathcal{D}_{\text{G}}
i.e.
\mathcal{D}_{\text{G}}\sigma=0
if and only if
\sigma=\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}_{x}f
, where
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
.

In more down-to-Earth terms, the second property says that the classical Grothendieck connection selects those sections which are global.

Finally, we can clarify the relation between
\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}
and the Grothendieck connection. The components
\Big(\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}^{i}\Big)_{j}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})
and
\Big(\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}^{i}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})
are given by the components of the classical Grothendieck connection
R^{i}_{j}(x;y)
and
R^{i}_{\bar{j}}(x;y)
evaluated in the second argument at
\hat{\mathbf{X}}
.

Having set up all the necessary tools, we can compute how
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}}
varies when we change the background
x\in M
. On a closed manifold, we have

(4.2.5)

d_{M}\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}}=-(\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R},\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}}),

which follows from the Grothendieck connection and that
\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}_{x}\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3}}
.

The above identities can be collected in a nicer way via the following definition.

Definition 4.2.3
.

(Formal global action) The
formal global action
for the model is defined by

(4.2.6)

\begin{split}\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}&\coloneqq\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}d\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j}+\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{j}+\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)\\
&=\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}}+\underbrace{\mathcal{S}_{R}+\mathcal{S}_{\bar{R}}}_{\coloneqq\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R}}.\end{split}

By using the formal global action, the
differential Classical Master Equation
(dCME) is satisfied

(4.2.7)

d_{M}\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3},x},\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3},x})=0.

Remark 4.2.4
.

Note that
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}
is an inhomogeneous form over
M
, where
\hat{S}_{\Sigma_{3}}
is a 0-form and
S_{\Sigma_{3},x,R}
is a 1-form. Therefore, Eq. (
4.2.7
) has a 0-form, a 1-form and a 2-form part. Specifically, the 0-form part

(4.2.8)

(\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}},\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}})=0,

is the usual CME. The 1-form part:

(4.2.9)

d_{M}\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}}+(\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R},\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}})=0,

means that
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3}}
is a global object (see Remark
4.2.2
). The 2-form part

(4.2.10)

d_{M}\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R},\mathcal{S}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R})=0,

means that the operator
\mathcal{D}_{G}
is flat connection (see Eq. (
4.2.4
)). Explicitly, we have

(4.2.11)

\displaystyle d_{x}\mathcal{S}_{R}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{S}_{R},\mathcal{S}_{R})=0,

(4.2.12)

\displaystyle d_{x}\mathcal{S}_{\bar{R}}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{S}_{R},\mathcal{S}_{\bar{R}})=0,

(4.2.13)

\displaystyle d_{\bar{x}}\mathcal{S}_{R}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\bar{R}},\mathcal{S}_{R})=0,

(4.2.14)

\displaystyle d_{\bar{x}}\mathcal{S}_{\bar{R}}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{S}_{\bar{R}},\mathcal{S}_{\bar{R}})=0.

Let
\Sigma_{3}
be (again) a manifold with boundary. The BV-BFV theory on
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}
furnishes the cohomological vector field
Q_{\Sigma_{3},x}
. Moreover, by using the lift of
\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}
, we can define

(4.2.15)

\tilde{Q}_{\Sigma_{3},x}=Q_{\Sigma_{3},x}+\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}.

Then, the
modified differential Classical Master Equation
(mdCME) is satisfied:

(4.2.16)

\iota_{\tilde{Q}_{\Sigma_{3},x}}\omega_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\delta\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}+\pi^{*}\alpha^{\partial}_{\partial\Sigma_{3},x},

where

(4.2.17)

\begin{split}\tilde{Q}_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(-d\hat{\mathbf{X}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}}&-\Omega^{pq}\frac{\delta\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})}{\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{p}}\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{j}\frac{\delta}{\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{q}}-\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})dx^{j}\Omega_{ip}\frac{\delta}{\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{p}}\\
&-\Omega^{pq}\frac{\delta\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})}{\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{p}}\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{\bar{j}}\frac{\delta}{\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{q}}-\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})dx^{\bar{j}}\Omega_{ip}\frac{\delta}{\delta\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{p}}\bigg).\end{split}

In preparation for the comparisons we will draw in the following section, we redefine the components
\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}
and
\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}
by a multiplicative factor
1/k!
as

(4.2.18)

\begin{split}\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})&=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\hat{R}^{i}_{j;j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}(x)\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j_{1}}\dots\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j_{k}},\\
\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})&=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\hat{R}^{i}_{{\bar{j}};j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}(x)\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j_{1}}\dots\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j_{k}}.\end{split}

5.
Comparison with the original Rozansky–Witten model

In this section, we show that the globalized model we have just constructed reduces to the RW model (Section
3
) and, moreover it provides a globalization of the former.

In order to compare effectively these models, we need to be more explicit about the terms involved in the classical Grothendieck connection. First, we discuss the choice of holomorphic formal exponential map in more detail. Since our target is a symplectic manifold, we choose the formal exponential map which preserves the symplectic form considered in
[
QZ11
]
and we adapt it to our case, i.e.

(5.0.1)

\varphi^{i}=x^{i}+y^{i}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{i}_{jk}y^{j}y^{k}+\bigg\{-\frac{1}{6}\partial_{c}\Gamma^{i}_{jk}+\frac{1}{3}\Gamma^{i}_{mc}\Gamma^{m}_{jk}-\frac{1}{24}R^{i}_{cjk}\bigg\}y^{c}y^{j}y^{k}+O(y^{4}),

where
R^{i}_{cjk}=(\Omega^{-1})^{bi}R^{\hskip 5.69054pta}_{bc\;k}\Omega_{aj}
.

The Grothendieck connection is then

(5.0.2)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}=dx^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}+dx^{\bar{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\bar{i}}}+dx^{j}\Big(R_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}+dx^{\bar{j}}\Big(R_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}},

where the third term on the right hand side was computed in
[
QZ11
]
,

(5.0.3)

\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}dx^{j}=-\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y}\bigg)^{-1}\bigg]^{i}_{p}\frac{\partial\varphi^{p}}{\partial x^{j}}dx^{j}=-\bigg[dx^{j}\bigg(\delta^{i}_{j}+\Gamma^{i}_{kj}y^{k}-\bigg(\frac{1}{8}R^{\hskip 4.2679pti}_{j\;ks}+\frac{1}{4}R^{\hskip 7.11317pti}_{jk\;s}\bigg)y^{k}y^{s}\bigg)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}+\dots\bigg],

whereas the fourth term is

(5.0.4)

\begin{split}\Big(\hat{R}^{i}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}&=-\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y}\bigg)^{-1}\bigg]^{i}_{p}\frac{\partial\varphi^{p}}{\partial x^{\bar{j}}}dx^{\bar{j}}=\bigg[-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{p}_{ab,\bar{j}}y^{a}y^{b}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg][\delta^{i}_{p}+\dots]=-\bigg[-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{i}_{ab,\bar{j}}y^{a}y^{b}dx^{\bar{j}}+\dots\bigg]\\
&=R^{i}_{ab\bar{j}}y^{a}y^{b}dx^{\bar{j}}-\dots.\end{split}

Considering the terms coming from the classical Grothendieck connection and the redefinition (
4.2.18
), we can re-write the formal global action (
4.2.6
) as

(5.0.5)

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}d\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{i}_{jk}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{k}\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{j}-\delta^{i}_{j}\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{j}+\dots+\frac{1}{3!}R^{i}_{ks\bar{j}}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{k}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{s}\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{\bar{j}}+\dots\bigg).

For convenience, we recall the RW action
[
RW97
]

(5.0.6)

S_{\text{RW}}=\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\bigg(\Omega_{IJ}\chi_{\mu}^{I}\nabla_{\nu}\chi_{\rho}^{J}-\frac{1}{3}\Omega_{IJ}R^{J}_{KL\bar{M}}\chi_{\mu}^{I}\chi_{\nu}^{K}\chi_{\rho}^{L}\eta^{\bar{M}}+\frac{1}{3}(\nabla_{L}\Omega_{IK})(\partial_{\mu}\phi^{I}_{\perp})\chi_{\nu}^{K}\chi_{\rho}^{L}\bigg).

If we assume that the connection is compatible with the symplectic form, the third term in the RW action (
5.0.6
) drops. We are left with the first two terms. By associating
\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\leftrightarrow\chi^{I}
and
dx^{\bar{j}}\leftrightarrow\eta^{\bar{M}}
, we can sum up the comparison in Table
5.0.1
.

Kinetic term

Interaction term

Original RW model

\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{h}}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}\Omega_{IJ}\chi_{\mu}^{I}\nabla_{\nu}\chi_{\rho}^{J}

-\frac{1}{3!}\Omega_{IJ}R^{J}_{KL\bar{M}}\chi_{\mu}^{I}\chi_{\nu}^{K}\chi_{\rho}^{L}\eta^{\bar{M}}

Our model

\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}d\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j}-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{i}_{jk}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{k}\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{j}

\frac{1}{3!}R^{i}_{ks\bar{j}}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{k}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{s}\Omega_{il}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{l}dx^{\bar{j}}

Table 5.0.1
.
Comparison between kinetic term and interaction term for the RW theory and our model.

The sign discrepancy comes from having defined the connection as
\nabla=d-\Gamma
which gives a negative sign in front of the
\Gamma^{i}_{jk}
(see Eq. (
5.0.1
)).

Moreover, when the curvature misses the
(2,0)
-part (which could happen when we have a Hermitian metric), the remaining terms in our model are just the perturbative expansion of
R^{i}_{ks\bar{j}}
around
x
. If we cut off the expansion at the first order, we are left with the original RW model.

6.
Comparison with other globalization constructions

In the next sections, we are going to compare our globalization model with other three constructions: the first by
[
CLL17
]

uses tools of derived geometry to linearize the space of fields in the neighbourhood of a constant map as well as the Fedosov connection
[
Fey49
]
, the second
[
Ste17
]
is an extension to a manifold with boundary of the first procedure, while the third
[
QZ09
,
QZ10
,
KQZ13
]
uses an approach similar to ours.

6.1.
Comparison with the CLL construction

We compare our model with the formulation of the RW model constructed in
[
CLL17
]
in the setting of derived geometry (see Appendix
C
).

Let
\Sigma_{3}
be a closed
3
dimensional manifold and
M
be a holomorphic symplectic manifold with a non-degenerate holomorphic
2
-form
\omega
.

To determine fields we use the language of
L_{\infty}
-spaces (see
[
Cos11
,
Cos11a
]
for an introduction) and we define the the space of fields as

(6.1.1)

\Maps({\Sigma_{3}}_{,\,\mathrm{dR}},M_{\bar{\partial}}),

where
{\Sigma_{3}}_{,\,\mathrm{dR}}
is the elliptic ringed space equipped with a sheaf of differential forms over
\Sigma_{3}
, i.e.
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma_{3})
and
M_{\bar{\partial}}=(M,\mathfrak{g}_{M})
is a sheaf of
L_{\infty}
-algebras, where
\mathfrak{g}_{M}=\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)\otimes T^{1,0}M[-1]
with
T^{1,0}M
the holomorphic tangent bundle.
Since the critical points of the action functional are constant maps from
\Sigma_{3}
to
M
, we are going to study
\Maps({\Sigma_{3}}_{,\,\mathrm{dR}},M_{\bar{\partial}})
in the neighbourhood of a constant map
x\in\Maps({\Sigma_{3}}_{,\,\mathrm{dR}},M_{\bar{\partial}})
, namely

(6.1.2)

\mathcal{F}_{\text{CLL}}\coloneqq\hbox{\hskip with
\hbox{\hskip }{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}({\Sigma_{3}}_{,\,\mathrm{dR}},M_{\bar{\partial}})
defined as in
[
Cos11
,
Cos11a
]
.

Having specified the space of fields, the shifted symplectic structure is given by

(6.1.3)

\begin{split}\braket{-,-}:\ &\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{CLL}}\otimes_{\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)}\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{CLL}}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)[-1]\\
&\braket{\alpha\otimes g_{1},\beta\otimes g_{2}}:=\underbrace{\omega(g_{1},g_{2})}_{\text{sympl. struct. on $M$}}\,\,\,\int_{\Sigma_{3}}\alpha\wedge\beta,\end{split}

where
\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)=\Gamma\left(\bigwedge^{\bullet}T^{\vee}M\right)
is a section of the cotangent bundle.

Since
C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}_{M}):=\hbox{\hskip {\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}_{\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)}(\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}_{M}[1])=\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)}\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}_{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)}(T^{\vee 1,0}M)
, to construct the action functional and to find our
L_{\infty}
-algebra we can use a procedure similar to the Fedosov’s construction of a connection on a symplectic manifold
[
Fed94
]
.
Let us denote the sections of the
holomorphic Weyl bundle
on
M
by

(6.1.4)

\mathcal{W}=\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)}\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee 1,0}M)[\![\hbar]\!]

where
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee 1,0}M)[\![\hbar]\!]
is the completed symmetric algebra over
T^{\vee 1,0}M
, the holomorphic cotangent bundle which has a local basis
\{y^{i}\}
with respect to the local holomorphic coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
. We call the sub-bundle
\Omega^{p,q}(M)\otimes\Sym^{r}(T^{\vee 1,0}M)
of
\mathcal{W}
its
(p,q,r)
component, in particular we refer to
r
as
weight
. To
\hbar
is assigned a weight of 2.

Proposition 6.1.1

(
[
CLL17
]
)
.

There is a connection on the holomorphic Weyl bundle of the following form

(6.1.5)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{F}}=\nabla-\delta+\frac{1}{\hbar}[I,-]_{\mathcal{W}},

which is flat modulo
\hbar
and
[-,-]_{\mathcal{W}}
is defined as in
[
CLL17
]
. Here
I
is a
1
-form valued section of the Weyl bundle of weight
\geq 3
, i.e.
I\in\bigoplus_{r\geq 3}\Gamma(\Sym^{r}(T^{\vee 1,0}M)\otimes T^{\vee}M)
,
\nabla
is the extension to
\mathcal{W}
of a connection on
T^{1,0}M
which is compatible with the complex structure as well as with the holomorphic symplectic form and torsion free,
\delta=dx^{i}\wedge\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}
is an operator on
\mathcal{W}
.

The connection
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{F}}
is called
Fedosov connection
and it provides the
L_{\infty}
-structure on
\mathfrak{g}_{M}
. In these terms
the action can be written as

(6.1.6)

\mathcal{S}_{\text{CLL}}=\frac{1}{2}\braket{d_{\Sigma_{3}}\alpha,\alpha}+\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\braket{\ell_{k}(\alpha^{\otimes k}),\alpha}

with
\alpha\in\mathcal{F}_{\text{CLL}}
,
\braket{-,-}
defined as in (
6.1.3
) and
\ell_{k}
are the higher brackets in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra, and
d_{\Sigma_{3}}
the de Rham differential on the source
\Sigma_{3}
. We can read
\ell_{0}
from the Fedosov connection in (
6.1.5
), i.e.

(6.1.7)

\ell_{0}=-dx^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}

The
L_{\infty}
-products
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are computed in the next section, when we compare the Fedosov connection with the classical Grothendieck connection.

Remark 6.1.2
.

The action in (
6.1.6
) satisfies the CME
(\mathcal{S}_{\text{CLL}},\mathcal{S}_{\text{CLL}})=0
(see
[
CLL17
, Proposition 2.16]
). Moreover, in
[
CLL17
]
it was observed that this construction is the formal version of the original RW model in the case when the
(2,0)
-part of the curvature is zero (see
[
CLL17
, Section 2.3]
).

6.1.1.
Comparison between the Fedosov connection and the classical Grothendieck connection

The sufficient condition for the flatness of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{F}}
(see the proof of Proposition
6.1.1
in
[
CLL17
]
) implies that
I
satisfies

(6.1.8)

I=\delta^{-1}(R+\nabla I)+\frac{1}{\hbar}\delta^{-1}I^{2},

where
\delta^{-1}=y^{i}\cdot\iota_{\partial_{x^{i}}}
(up to a normalization factor) is another operator on
\mathcal{W}
and
R
is the curvature tensor.

Remark 6.1.3
.

Since
I
is a
1
-form valued section of
\mathcal{W}
, we can decompose it into its holomorphic and antiholomorphic component respectively. In particular the antiholomoprhic part component is the Taylor expansion of the Atiyah class as noted in
[
CLL17
]
. In the case
R^{2,0}=0
, the
L_{\infty}
-algebra is fully encoded by Taylor expansion of the Atiyah class as first noted by Kapranov in
[
Kap99
]
.

Since the operator
\delta^{-1}
increases the weight by
1
, while
\nabla
preserves the weight and
I
has at least weight
3
, we can find a solution of the above equation with the following leading term (cubic term)
16
16

16

See also
[
Fed00
,
GLL17
]
.
:

(6.1.9)

\delta^{-1}R=\frac{1}{8}\big[-\Gamma_{ijk,r}+\Gamma_{sir}\Gamma_{pjk}\Omega^{sp}\big]y^{i}y^{j}y^{r}dx^{k}+\frac{1}{6}R_{\bar{k}rij}y^{i}y^{j}y^{r}dx^{\bar{k}}=\delta^{-1}R_{t}+\delta^{-1}\bar{R}.

Since the Fedosov connection requires the computation of
\frac{1}{\hbar}[I,-]_{\mathcal{W}}
, we compute this commutator for the leading order term of
I
, which is the cubic term we have just found. For the first term on the right hand side of Eq. (
6.1.9
) we have

(6.1.10)

\begin{split}\frac{1}{\hbar}[\delta^{-1}R_{t},-]_{\mathcal{W}}&=\bigg[\frac{1}{8}\bigg(-\Gamma_{rjk,q}+\Gamma_{srq}\Gamma_{pjk}\Omega^{sp}\bigg)+\frac{1}{4}\bigg(-\Gamma_{qjk,r}+\Gamma_{sqk}\Gamma_{pjr}\Omega^{sp}\bigg)\bigg]\Omega^{qi}y^{j}y^{r}dx^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}\\
&=\bigg[\frac{1}{8}\bigg(-\Omega_{mr}\Gamma^{m}_{jk,q}+\Omega_{mr}\Gamma^{m}_{sk}\Gamma_{pjk}\Omega^{sp}\bigg)+\frac{1}{4}\bigg(-\Omega_{mq}\Gamma^{m}_{jk,r}+\Omega_{mq}\Gamma^{m}_{sk}\Gamma_{pjr}\Omega^{sp}\bigg)\bigg]\times\\
&\hskip 284.52756pt\times\Omega^{qi}y^{j}y^{r}dx^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}\\
&=\bigg[\frac{1}{8}R^{\hskip 4.97922pti}_{k\ rj}+\frac{1}{4}R^{\hskip 8.25128pti}_{kr\,j}\bigg]y^{j}y^{r}dx^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}.\end{split}

For the second term we have

(6.1.11)

\frac{1}{\hbar}\big[\delta^{-1}\bar{R},-\big]_{\mathcal{W}}=\frac{1}{2}\Gamma^{i}_{jk,\bar{r}}y^{j}y^{k}dz^{\bar{r}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}.

After renaming some indices, the Fedosov connection is then

(6.1.12)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{F}}=d_{x}+d_{\bar{x}}-dx^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}-dx^{j}\Gamma^{i}_{kj}y^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}+dx^{j}\bigg(\frac{1}{8}R^{\hskip 4.2679pti}_{j\;ks}+\frac{1}{4}R^{\hskip 7.11317pti}_{jk\;s}\bigg)y^{k}y^{s}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}+\frac{1}{2}dx^{\bar{j}}\Gamma^{i}_{ks,\bar{j}}y^{k}y^{s}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}+\dots.

More explicitly,

(6.1.13)

\begin{split}\ell_{1}&=-dx^{j}\Gamma^{i}_{kj}y^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}},\\
\ell_{2}&=dx^{j}\bigg(\frac{1}{8}R^{\hskip 4.2679pti}_{j\;ks}+\frac{1}{4}R^{\hskip 7.11317pti}_{jk\;s}\bigg)y^{k}y^{s}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}+\frac{1}{2}dx^{\bar{j}}\Gamma^{i}_{ks,\bar{j}}y^{k}y^{s}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}.\end{split}

Remark 6.1.4
.

The first terms in the Fedosov connection, explicitly written in (
6.1.12
) coincide with the first terms for the classical Grothendieck connection (
5.0.2
). Furthermore, by substituting the explicit expressions of
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
in the action
\mathcal{S}_{\text{CLL}}
(
6.1.6
), we can see that it coincides with the action
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}
(
5.0.5
).

6.1.2.
Comparison between the CLL space of fields and globalization space of fields

By rephrasing the argument of
[
Mos20
, Section 6.1]
to our context, we can extend the classical Grothendieck connection
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
to the complex

(6.1.14)

\Gamma\bigg(\bigwedge\nolimits^{\bullet}T^{\vee}M\otimes\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee 1,0}M)\bigg).

which is the algebra of functions on the formal graded manifold

(6.1.15)

T[1]M\bigoplus T^{1,0}M.

This graded manifold is turned into a differential graded manifold by the classical Grothendieck connection
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
vanishes on the body of the graded manifold, we can linearize at
x\in M
and we get

(6.1.16)

T_{x}[1]M\bigoplus T_{x}^{1,0}M.

On this graded manifold, we have a curved
L_{\infty}
-structure (which is the same as
\mathfrak{g}_{M}[1]
) and Eq. (
6.1.14
) can be interpreted as the Chevalley–Eilenberg complex of the aforementioned
L_{\infty}
-algebra. Then, the space of fields for the globalized theory can be rewritten as

(6.1.17)

\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma_{3})\otimes\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)\otimes T^{1,0}_{x}M

which is the same as
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{CLL}}
by linearizing at
x\in M
the holomorphic tangent bundle as
\mathcal{D}_{\text{F}}
vanishes on
M
.

Remark 6.1.5
.

The idea that the classical Grothendieck connection and the Fedosov connection coincide is not new, in particular see
[
CMW19
, Remark 3.6]
and
[
CLL17
, Section 2.3]
.

Remark 6.1.6
.

Finally note that in
[
CLL17
]
the source manifold
\Sigma_{3}
was considered to be a closed manifold. As explained above (see Section
4.2
) our construction is valid also when
\partial\Sigma_{3}\neq\emptyset
. In the next section, we tackle this last setting by comparing our approach with
[
Ste17
]
, where the derived geometric framework was implemented for manifolds with boundary.

6.2.
Comparison with Steffens’ construction

In
[
Ste17
]
, Steffens applied the same derived geometry approach we have seen in the last section to what he calls
AKSZ theories of Chern–Simons type
: CS theory and RW theories. In particular, his BV formulation of the RW model is completely analogue to the one in
[
CLL17
]
: same space of fields,
L_{\infty}
-algebra, action, etc. However, he takes a step further. He proves a
formal AKSZ theorem

[
Ste17
, Theorem 2.4.1]
in the context of derived geometry. His RW model is then shown to be an AKSZ theory by attaching degree 2 to the holomorphic symplectic form (as we did ourselves in Section
4
). Consequently, he provides a BV-BFV formulation for the RW model. The BFV action found in
[
Ste17
]
is analogous to the action in (
6.1.6
) in one dimension less (as it is customary with AKSZ theories). Even if the
L_{\infty}
products are not explicit in his construction, by using the ones in (
6.1.13
), his BV-BFV formulation of the RW model is visibly identical to ours.

6.3.
Comparison with the (K)QZ construction

Let
\Sigma_{3}
be a 3-dimensional manifold and
M
a hyperKähler manifold with holomorphic symplectic form
\Omega
. Consider the symplectic graded manifold
\mathcal{M}\coloneqq T^{\vee 0,1}[2]T^{\vee 0,1}[1]M
constructed out of
M
. It has the following coordinates:
X^{i},X^{\bar{i}}
of degree 0 parametrizing
M
,
V^{\bar{i}}
of degree 1 parametrizing the fiber
T^{0,1}M
and dual coordinates
P_{\bar{i}},Q_{\bar{i}}
of degree 2 and 1, respectively. The symplectic form is

(6.3.1)

\omega_{\mathcal{M}}=dP_{\bar{i}}\wedge dX^{\bar{i}}+dQ_{\bar{i}}\wedge dV^{\bar{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}dX^{i}\wedge dX^{j}.

In order to have a ghost degree 2 symplectic form, the authors assign degree 2 to
\Omega
. With this setup, in
[
QZ10
,
QZ09
,
KQZ13
]
, Källén, Qiu and Zabzine construct an AKSZ model

(6.3.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\text{QZ}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Maps(T[1]\Sigma_{3},T^{\vee 0,1}[2]T^{\vee 0,1}[1]M)

(6.3.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\text{QZ}}

\displaystyle=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}d^{3}zd^{3}\theta\bigg(\mathbf{P}_{\bar{i}}D\mathbf{X}^{\bar{i}}+\mathbf{Q}_{\bar{i}}D\mathbf{V}^{\bar{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\mathbf{X}^{i}D\mathbf{X}^{j}+\mathbf{P}_{\bar{i}}\mathbf{V}^{\bar{i}}\bigg)

endowed with a cohomological vector field

(6.3.4)

Q=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}d^{3}zd^{3}\theta\bigg(D\mathbf{P}_{\bar{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{P}_{\bar{i}}}+D\mathbf{Q}_{\bar{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{Q}_{\bar{i}}}+D\mathbf{V}^{\bar{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{V}^{\bar{i}}}+D\mathbf{X}^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{X}^{i}}+D\mathbf{X}^{\bar{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{X}^{\bar{i}}}+\mathbf{P}_{\bar{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{Q}_{\bar{i}}}+\mathbf{V}^{\bar{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mathbf{X}^{\bar{i}}}\bigg),

where to the source manifold
T[1]\Sigma_{3}
, we assign coordinates
\{z^{i}\}
of ghost degree 0 and coordinates
\{\theta^{i}\}
of degree 1.

Remark 6.3.1
.

With a suitable gauge-fixing consisting on a particular choice of Lagrangian submanifolds, the action
\mathcal{S}_{\text{QZ}}
reduces to the RW model up to a factor of
\hbar
(see
[
QZ09
, Section 4]
):

(6.3.5)

\mathcal{S}_{\text{QZ}}\bigg|_{\text{GF}}=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{3}z\bigg(\Omega_{ij}X^{i}_{(1)}\wedge d^{\nabla}X^{j}_{(1)}-\frac{1}{3}R_{k\bar{k}j}^{i}X^{k}_{(1)}\wedge\Omega_{li}X^{l}_{(1)}\wedge X^{j}_{(1)}V^{\bar{k}}_{(0)}\bigg),

with
d^{\nabla}X^{i}_{(1)}=dX^{i}_{(1)}+\Gamma^{i}_{jk}dX^{j}_{(0)}X^{k}_{(1)}
. Note that the only fields left are the even scalar
X^{i}_{(0)}
, the odd 1-form
X^{i}_{(1)}
and the odd scalar
V^{\bar{k}}_{(0)}
. A quick glance to our expression for the RW model in (
5.0.5
) (assume again the
(2,0)
part of the curvature is zero as well as we cut off the perturbative expansion of the
(1,1)
part at the
R^{i}_{ks\bar{j}}
) suggests the association
V^{\bar{k}}_{(0)}\Leftrightarrow dx^{\bar{k}}
. We will comment more on this later.

By expanding
\mathbf{X}^{i}
through the geodesic exponential map and by pulling back
\omega_{\mathcal{M}}
as well as
S_{\text{QZ}}
through it, the authors find

(6.3.6)

\displaystyle\exp^{*}\omega_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=dP_{\bar{i}}\wedge dX^{\bar{i}}+dQ_{\bar{i}}\wedge dV^{\bar{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}(x)dy^{i}\wedge dy^{j}-\delta X^{\bar{i}}\delta\Theta_{\bar{i}}

(6.3.7)

\displaystyle\exp^{*}\mathcal{S}_{\text{QZ}}\bigg|_{\tilde{\mathbf{P}}}

\displaystyle=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}d^{3}zd^{3}\theta\bigg(\tilde{{\mathbf{P}}}_{\bar{i}}D\mathbf{X}^{\bar{i}}+\mathbf{Q}_{\bar{i}}D\mathbf{V}^{\bar{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\mathbf{y}^{i}D\mathbf{y}^{j}-\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{\bar{i}}\mathbf{V}^{\bar{i}}+\Theta_{\bar{i}}(x;\mathbf{y})\mathbf{V}^{\bar{i}}\bigg)

where
\Theta_{\bar{i}}
is of degree 2 and given by

(6.3.8)

\Theta_{\bar{i}}(x;y)=\sum^{\infty}_{n=3}\frac{1}{n!}\nabla_{l_{4}}\dots\nabla_{l_{n}}R^{\hskip 8.53581ptk}_{\bar{i}l_{1}\ l_{3}}\Omega_{kl_{2}}(x)y^{l_{1}}\dots y^{l_{n}}

and
\tilde{P}_{\bar{i}}:=P_{\bar{i}}+\Theta_{\bar{i}}
.

After removing the
spectator fields
(see
[
QZ10
,
KQZ13
]
), the action becomes

(6.3.9)

\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}d^{3}zd^{3}\theta\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\mathbf{y}^{i}D\mathbf{y}^{j}+\Theta_{\bar{i}}(x;\mathbf{y})\mathbf{V}^{\bar{i}}\bigg),

which further reduces to

(6.3.10)

\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}d^{3}zd^{3}\theta\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ij}\mathbf{y}^{i}D\mathbf{y}^{j}+\Theta_{\bar{i}}(x;\mathbf{y})V^{\bar{i}}_{(0)}\bigg)

for degree reasons (
V^{\bar{i}}_{(0)}
is an odd scalar). This action fails the CME by a
\bar{\partial}
-exact term due to
\Theta
satisfying the Maurer–Cartan equation

(6.3.11)

\bar{\partial}_{[\bar{i}}\Theta_{\bar{j}]}=-(\Theta_{\bar{i}},\Theta_{\bar{j}}),

where
\bar{\partial}
is the Dolbeault differential and
[\bar{i}\bar{j}]
denotes antisymmetrization over the indices
\bar{i}
and
\bar{j}
.

The hyperKähler structure is then relaxed. A new connection which still preserves
\Omega
(crucial for the perturbative approach through the exponential map above) is found. However, since the connection is not Hermitian, the curvature of
\Gamma
exhibits also a
(2,0)
-component. This complicates the exponential map which can not be worked out at all orders as in (
6.3.8
). In
[
KQZ13
]
, the authors argue that a solution to this problem should originate from principles related to the globalization issues discussed in
[
BLN02
]
and the application of Fedosov connection in order to deal with perturbation theory on curved manifold
[
CF01
]
. In the realm of this paper, we furnish an affirmative answer to both their ideas. In particular, as we have seen in Section
6.1
, the Fedosov connection allowed to compute the terms in the
L_{\infty}
-algebra and thus to work out the exponential map. In Section
4.2
, we have seen the Grothendieck connection to accomplish the same in the context of formal geometry.

Remark 6.3.2
.

We can compare the procedure above with our globalization construction by associating
V^{\bar{i}}_{(0)}
with
dx^{\bar{i}}
. First, note that
\Big(R_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{i}}
in Eq. (
6.3.8
) matches with the second term in Eq. (
4.2.1
). Second, the action in (
6.3.10
) coincides with our globalized action in (
4.2.6
) if we “forget” the
(2,0)
-part of the curvature. In particular, by associating
\bar{\partial}
with
dx^{\bar{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\bar{i}}}
, we can interpret the failure of (
6.3.10
) to satisfy the CME due to the term (
6.3.11
) as a consequence of the action satisfying the
(1,1)
-part of the dCME (Eq. (
4.2.7
)).

We reserve the last remark of the section to precise the association between
V^{\bar{i}}_{(0)}
and
dx^{\bar{i}}
as well as their “meaning” as we promised in Remark
6.3.1
.

Remark 6.3.3
.

As we have seen above,
V^{\bar{i}}_{(0)}
and
dx^{\bar{i}}
arise in two different contexts: the first is an odd scalar coordinate parametrizing the fibers of
T^{0,1}M
, while the second is introduced through the classical Grothendieck connection as well as the perturbative expansion.

Nevertheless, the association makes sense considering that
V^{\bar{i}}_{(0)}
is interpreted as an odd harmonic zero mode in
[
KQZ13
]
. In fact, recall from Section
4.1
,
x
is the zero mode obtained from the Euler–Lagrange equation
D\mathbf{X}=0
. If we enlarge the complex (see Eq. (
6.1.14
)), the space of fields becomes (
6.1.17
) meaning that
dx^{\bar{i}}\in T^{\vee 0,1}M
, i.e. an odd zero mode. This association was pointed out first by Qiu and Zabzine in
[
QZ12
]
.

The presence of these quantities has been known in the literature since the early days of the RW model and has deep consequences. Since they are odd, there can be as many as the dimension of
M
. As such, the perturbative expansion can not be infinite, but it can only stop at a certain order. This is a crucial difference between the CS and the RW theory, which was originally spotted in
[
RW97
]
and attributed to the need for the RW theory to saturate the zero modes. According to Kontsevich in
[
Kon99
]
, as a result the RW model can be understood as an AKSZ model with “parameters” (these parameters are
V^{\bar{i}}_{(0)}
or
dx^{\bar{i}}
). In the same article, he presented a different perspective on this subject by pointing out that the RW invariants come from characteristic classes of holomorphic connections.

7.
BF
-like formulation of the Rozansky–Witten model

In order to quantize our globalized version of the RW model in the quantum BV-BFV framework
[
CMR17
]
, we need to formulate the model as a
BF
-like theory. This can be done by exploiting the similarities between the RW theory and the CS theory. These similarities have also been crucial in the construction of
[
Ste17
]
. There it was argued that RW could be split following a similar approach to the one of Cattaneo, Mnev and Wernli for the CS theory in
[
CMW17
]
(see also
[
Wer18
]
for a more detailed exposition).

As shown in
[
CLL17
]
(see Eq. (
6.1.3
)), we have a pairing on
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}
given by the BV symplectic form which can be defined on homogeneous elements
\hat{\mathbf{Y}}\otimes g_{1}
and
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}\otimes g_{2}
as

(7.0.1)

\begin{split}\braket{-,-}:\ &\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}\otimes\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M),\\
&\braket{\hat{\mathbf{Y}}\otimes g_{1},\hat{\mathbf{Z}}\otimes g_{2}}:=\underbrace{\Omega(g_{1},g_{2})}_{\text{sympl. struct. on $M$}}\,\,\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\hat{\mathbf{Y}}\wedge\hat{\mathbf{Z}}\bigg).\end{split}

By expanding
\hat{\mathbf{X}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}
as
\hat{\mathbf{X}}=\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}e_{i}
, we have

(7.0.2)

\braket{\hat{\mathbf{X}},\hat{\mathbf{X}}}=\Omega(e_{i},e_{j})\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\wedge\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j}\bigg)=\int_{T[1]\Sigma_{3}}\mu_{\Sigma_{3}}\bigg(\Omega_{ij}\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{i}\wedge\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{j}\bigg).

We can rewrite the globalized action (
4.2.6
) in the same way as in
[
CLL17
]
(see the action in (
6.1.6
)), we have

(7.0.3)

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{\Sigma_{3},x}=\frac{1}{2}\Big\langle\hat{\mathbf{X}},D\hat{\mathbf{X}}\Big\rangle+\Big\langle\Big(\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})dx^{j},\hat{\mathbf{X}}\Big\rangle+\Big\langle\Big(\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x;\hat{\mathbf{X}})dx^{\bar{j}},\hat{\mathbf{X}}\Big\rangle,

with

(7.0.4)

\begin{split}\Big(\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{j}(x,\hat{\mathbf{X}})&=\sum^{\infty}_{k=0}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}(\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{\otimes k}),\\
\Big(\hat{R}_{\Sigma_{3}}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(x,\hat{\mathbf{X}})&=\sum^{\infty}_{k=2}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}(\hat{\mathbf{X}}^{\otimes k}).\end{split}

Now, similarly to the approach in
[
CMW17
]
, we assume that we can split the
L_{\infty}
-algebra as

(7.0.5)

\mathfrak{g}[1]=\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)\otimes T^{\vee 1,0}M=\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)\otimes V\oplus\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(M)\otimes W,

with
V
and
W
two isotropic subspaces. We identify
W\cong V^{\vee}
via the pairing (in particular thanks to the holomorphic symplectic form). Consequently, the superfield splits as
\hat{\mathbf{X}}=\hat{\mathbf{A}}+\hat{\mathbf{B}}=\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{i}\xi_{i}+\xi^{i}\hat{\mathbf{B}}_{i}
with
\xi_{i}\in V
and
\xi^{i}\in W
. Concerning the assignment of degrees, we make the following choices. Since
\Omega
has ghost degree
2
(and as such
\Omega^{-1}
has ghost degree
-2
), we assign total degree 0 to
\mathbf{A}^{i}
and
\xi_{i}
, total degree
2
to
B_{i}
and total degree
-2
to
\xi^{i}
. We refer to Table
7.0.1
for an explanation of the ghost degrees for the components of the superfields
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{i}
and
\hat{\mathbf{B}}_{i}
. Then
\hat{\mathbf{A}}^{i}\oplus\hat{\mathbf{B}}_{i}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma_{3})\oplus\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma_{3})[2]
, which is a
BF
-like theory.

A^{i}_{(2)}
