Table 9.2.1
.
Feynman rules for the globalization terms in the action (
7.0.7
).

Notice how the structure of the Feynman rules repeats similarly at each order (e.g. for
R_{0}
we have 2 Feynman rules with degrees 0 and 2, respectively, while for
R_{1}
we have 3 graphs with degrees 0, 2, 4). Hence, it is easy to understand how this works for higher order terms.
From there, one can notice that we have two types of vertices:

•

vertices which are 1-forms in
dx^{i}
: we will denote them by a black dot (
\bullet
) and refer to them as black vertices;

•

vertices which are 1-forms in
dx^{{\bar{i}}}
: we will denote them by a red dot (
\bullet
) and refer to them as red vertices.

In our computations, we will limit ourselves to the Feynman rules in Table
9.2.1
, these are already enough to get a feeling about what is going on and even understand the behaviour of higher order terms, when possible. By using the names in the Table, since
n=\textrm{I}+\textrm{II}+\textrm{III}+\textrm{IV}+\textrm{V}+\textrm{VI}+\textrm{VII}+\textrm{VIII}+\textrm{IX}+\textrm{X}+\textrm{XI}+\textrm{XII}+\textrm{XIII}
is the sum of the vertices, the degree counting produces the following equation

(9.2.3)

\begin{split}\textrm{I}+\textrm{II}+\textrm{III}+\textrm{IV}+\textrm{V}+\textrm{VI}+\textrm{VII}+\textrm{VIII}&+\textrm{IX}+\textrm{X}+\textrm{XI}+\textrm{XII}+\textrm{XIII}+2m-3=\\
&2\textrm{II}+2\textrm{IV}+4\textrm{V}+2\textrm{VII}+4\textrm{VIII}+6\textrm{IX}+2\textrm{XI}+4\textrm{XII}+6\textrm{XIII},\end{split}

where on the right hand side we are taking into account that in the
\mathbb{B}
-representation, the arrows leaving the globalization vertex have to stay inside the collapsing subgraph. If this is not the case, by the boundary conditions on the propagator
[
CMR17
]
, the result would be zero.

First, let us focus on the black vertices (i.e. vertices I–IX). The equation reduces to

(9.2.4)

3\textrm{I}+\textrm{II}+3\textrm{III}+\textrm{IV}-\textrm{V}+3\textrm{VI}+\textrm{VII}-\textrm{VIII}-3\textrm{IX}+2m-3=0.

The Feynman diagrams contributing to the BFV boundary operator are those whose vertices solve the equation (
9.2.4
). Hence, let us solve the equation case-by-case.
Up to one bulk vertex, with the Feynman rules I–IX we have one diagram (see Fig.
9.2.1
).

\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}

Figure 9.2.1
.
First graph with a single black vertex contributing to the BFV boundary operator.

From Fig.
9.2.1
, we notice that in order to have a degree 1 operator which satisfies the degree counting for higher order terms we need vertices with an even number of heads and tails. We show the first higher order contributions in Fig.
9.2.2
, while a general diagram contributing to the BFV operator
is exhibited in Fig.
9.2.3
.

\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}

(a)

\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}
\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}

(b)

Figure 9.2.2
.
Second and third graph with a single black vertex contributing to the BFV boundary operator.

\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}
\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}
…
…

Figure 9.2.3
.
A general Feynman diagram contributing to the BFV operator in the
\mathbb{B}
-representation up to one black bulk vertex.

Concerning the red vertices, the graphs contributing to the BFV operator up to one bulk vertex will start to appear from the vertices associated to the term
(R_{3})_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}
(coming from the thid term in the action
7.0.7
). Taking this into account, the general term of the diagrams with a red vertex is shown in Fig.
9.2.4
.

\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}
\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}
…
…

Figure 9.2.4
.
A general Feynman diagram contributing to the BFV operator in the
\mathbb{B}
-representation up to one red bulk vertex. In particular, the graph with a total number of 4 arrows (2 entering and 2 leaving the red vertex) is the first non-zero contribution.

These considerations prove the following proposition.

Proposition 9.2.1
.

Consider the globalized split RW model, in the
\mathbb{B}
-representation, the first contribution to
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{\mathrm{pert}}
is given by
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{(1)}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{1,0}+\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{0,1}
with

(9.2.5)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{1,0}&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 1,S_{1},\dots S_{k}\\
i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j_{1},\dots,j_{k}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{k}}{(k+S_{1}+\dots+S_{k})!}\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}}\Big(\hat{R}_{2k-1}\Big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{j;j_{1}\dots j_{k}}dx^{j}[\mathbb{B}_{i_{1}}\mathbb{B}_{S_{1}}]\dots\\
&\hskip 227.62204pt\times[\mathbb{B}_{i_{k}}\mathbb{B}_{S_{k}}]\frac{\delta^{|j_{1}+\dots+j_{k}|+|S_{1}|+\dots+|S_{k}|}}{\delta[\mathbb{B}_{j_{1}}\dots\mathbb{B}_{j_{k}}][\delta\mathbb{B}_{S_{1}}]\dots[\delta\mathbb{B}_{S_{k}}]}\\
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{0,1}&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq 2,S_{1},\dots S_{k}\\
i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j_{1},\dots,j_{k}\end{subarray}}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{k}}{(k+S_{1}+\dots+S_{k})!}\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}}\Big(\hat{R}_{2k-1}\Big)^{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}_{\bar{j};j_{1}\dots j_{k}}dx^{\bar{j}}[\mathbb{B}_{i_{1}}\mathbb{B}_{S_{1}}]\dots\\
&\hskip 227.62204pt\times[\mathbb{B}_{i_{k}}\mathbb{B}_{S_{k}}]\frac{\delta^{|j_{1}+\dots+j_{k}|+|S_{1}|+\dots+|S_{k}|}}{\delta[\mathbb{B}_{j_{1}}\dots\mathbb{B}_{j_{k}}][\delta\mathbb{B}_{S_{1}}]\dots[\delta\mathbb{B}_{S_{k}}]}.\end{split}

For
n>1
, the situation gets more complicated. We solve equation (
9.2.4
) numerically. Empirically, for an even number of bulk vertices, we witness the absence of solutions. This implies immediately
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{(2)}=0
.

In the case
n=3
, the number of Feynman diagrams for the vertices I–IX increases dramatically with respect to the
n=1
case. This increment is tamed since the necessity of having a degree 1 operator will decrease their number. However, we are not able to provide an explicit as well as general form for the BFV operator along the same lines as in Proposition
9.2.1
. We rely on examples which we show in Appendix
D
.

Remark 9.2.2
.

Here we are assuming that the dimension of our target manifold
M
is at least 4, if this would not be the case, then we would not have the 3 bulk vertices contribution to the BFV boundary operator. Hence, the number of bulk vertices allowed is bounded by the dimension of
M
. This was already noticed in
[
CMW20
]
. The difference here is that this reflects the “odd Grassmanian nature” of the RW model with respect to CS theory (see Remark
6.3.3
).

9.3.
BFV boundary operator in the
\mathbb{A}
-representation

In the
\mathbb{A}
-representation, the arrows coming from the globalized vertices are allowed to leave the collapsing subgraph. Therefore, our arguments about the degree counting are not valid here. However since the coboundary operator has a total degree 1, while
\mathbb{A}^{i}
has total degree 0, we can have at most 1 bulk vertex, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{A}}_{\mathrm{pert}}=\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{A}}_{1,0}+\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{A}}_{0,1}
with

(9.3.1)

\begin{split}\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{A}}_{1,0}&=\sum_{k\geq 0}\,\,\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma_{3}}\sum_{J_{1},\dots,J_{r},I_{1},\dots,I_{s}}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{|I_{1}|+\dots+|I_{s}|}}{(|I_{1}|+\dots+|I_{s}|)!}\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)^{I_{1}\dots I_{s}}_{j;J_{1}\dots J_{r}}dx^{j}\prod^{r+s=k+1}_{r=1,s=1}[\mathbb{A}^{J_{r}}]\frac{\delta^{|I_{s}|}}{\delta[\mathbb{A}^{I_{s}}]},\\
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{A}}_{0,1}&=\sum_{k\geq 3}\,\,\int_{\partial_{1}\Sigma_{3}}\sum_{J_{1},\dots,J_{r},I_{1},\dots,I_{s}}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{|I_{1}|+\dots+|I_{s}|}}{(|I_{1}|+\dots+|I_{s}|)!}\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)^{I_{1}\dots I_{s}}_{\bar{j};J_{1}\dots J_{r}}dx^{\bar{j}}\prod^{r+s=k+1}_{r=1,s=1}[\mathbb{A}^{J_{r}}]\frac{\delta^{|I_{s}|}}{\delta[\mathbb{A}^{I_{s}}]},\end{split}

where we label by the multiindex
J_{r}
the arrows emanating from a boundary vertex towards the globalized vertex, by the multiindex
I_{s}
the leaves emanating from the bulk vertex. The sum of
r
and
s
has to be
k+1
since these are the total number of arrows leaving and arriving at a globalized vertex
(R_{k})_{j}dx^{j}
(or
(R_{k})_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}
).

9.4.
Flatness of the qGBFV operator for the globalized split RW model

In this section, we prove that the qGBFV operator for the globalized split RW model squares to zero. The proof follows along the same lines as in
[
CMW19
]
, we will remark where there are differences and refer to their work when the procedure is identical. Before entering into the details of the proof, we should mention that their proof (and the proof of the mdQME) depends on two assumptions:
unimodularity
and
absence of hidden faces
(
anomaly-free
condition). The first means that tadpoles are not allowed. In the case of the globalized split RW model, we notice that this assumption is not needed since tadpoles vanish
[
RW97
]
.

Assumption 9.4.1
.

We assume that the globalized split RW model is
anomaly-free
, i.e. for every graph
\Gamma
, we have that

(9.4.1)

\int_{F_{\geq 3}}\omega_{\Gamma}=0,

where by
F_{\geq 3}
, we denote the union of the faces where at least three bulk vertices collapse in the bulk (also called
hidden faces

[
BC98
]
).

Remark 9.4.2
.

It is well known that Chern–Simons theory is
not
an anomaly-free theory
[
AS91
,
AS94
]
. The construction of the quantum theory there depends on the choice of gauge-fixing. The appearance of anomalies can be resolve by choosing a framing and framing-dependent counter terms for the gauge-fixing. A famous example of an anomaly-free theory is given by the Poisson sigma model
[
CF00
]
since by the result of Kontsevich
[
Kon03
]
any 2-dimensional theory is actually anomaly-free.
A general method for dealing with theories that do have anomalies is to add counter terms to the action. If the differential form
\omega_{\Gamma}
, which is integrated over the hidden faces, is exact, one can use the primitive form to cancel the anomalies by the additional vertices that appear.

Since the integrals we will consider are fiber integrals, we will apply of Stokes’ theorem for integration along a compact fiber with corners, i.e.

(9.4.2)

d\pi_{*}=\pi_{*}d-\pi^{\partial}_{*},

where
\pi_{*}
denotes the fiber integration.
In particular, the application of Stokes’ theorem to a fiber integral yiels

(9.4.3)

(d_{x}+d_{\bar{x}})\int_{\text{C}_{\Gamma}}\omega_{\Gamma}=\int_{\text{C}_{\Gamma}}(d+d_{\bar{x}})\omega_{\Gamma}-\int_{\partial\text{C}_{\Gamma}}\omega_{\Gamma},

where
d
is the differential on
M\times C_{\Gamma}
.

Theorem 9.4.3

(Flatness of the qGBFV operator)
.

The qGBFV operator
\nabla_{\textup{G}}
for the anomaly-free globalized split RW model squares to zero, i.e.

(9.4.4)

(\nabla_{\textup{G}})^{2}\equiv 0,

where

(9.4.5)

\nabla_{\textup{G}}=d_{M}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma_{3},x}}+\frac{i}{\hbar}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}=d_{x}+d_{\bar{x}}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma_{3},x}}+\frac{i}{\hbar}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}.

Proof.

According to
[
CMW19
]
, the flatness of
\nabla_{\mathrm{G}}
is equivalent to the equation

(9.4.6)

i\hbar d_{M}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}-\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}},\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}\bigg]=0.

This equation was proven for a globalized split AKSZ theory in
[
CMW19
]
, in which the
d_{M}
is just the de Rham differential on the body of the target manifold. However, in our case,
d_{M}
is the sum of the holomorphic and antiholomorphic Dolbeault differentials on
M
.

We prove Eq. (
9.4.6
) for
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}
. For
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{A}}
, the proof is analogous as discussed in
[
CMW19
]
. Suppose we apply
d_{M}
to a term of the form

(9.4.7)

\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{\Gamma}=\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}}\sigma_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}dx^{j};\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)^{I}_{J_{1}\dots J_{s}}[\mathbb{B}^{J_{1}}]\dots[\mathbb{B}^{J_{s}}]\frac{\delta}{\delta[\mathbb{B}^{I}]},

where
k
could be any number greater than 0. Here, we chose the easiest term to express with more clarity what is going on. As in
[
CMW19
]
, we apply Stokes’ theorem. However, this is different to the corresponding situation in
[
CMW19
]
since in our theory we have also red vertices
18
18

18

For the sake of clarity, we stress again that in
[
CMW19
]
, the vertices are only “black” since
d_{M}
is the de Rham differential on the body of the target manifold.
, which is portrayed by the fact that
\sigma_{\Gamma}
depends also on
\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}
. We obtain

(9.4.8)

\begin{split}(d_{x}+d_{\bar{x}})\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{\Gamma}=\int_{\partial_{2}\Sigma_{3}}\bigg\{(d_{x}+d_{\bar{x}})\sigma_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}dx^{j};\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)\bigg\}^{I}_{J_{1}\dots J_{s}}[\mathbb{B}^{J_{1}}]\dots[\mathbb{B}^{J_{s}}]\frac{\delta}{\delta[\mathbb{B}^{I}]}+[\Omega^{\mathbb{B}}_{0},\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{\Gamma}],\end{split}

where the second term is produced when
d_{x}
acts on the
\mathbb{B}
fields (we do not have a corresponding term for
d_{\bar{x}}
since we do not have fields
\mathbb{B}^{\bar{i}}
terms to act on). By applying again Stokes’ theorem, we have:

(9.4.9)

\begin{split}(d_{x}+d_{\bar{x}})\sigma_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}dx^{j};\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)&=(d_{x}+d_{\bar{x}})\int_{\tilde{\text{C}}_{\Gamma}}\omega_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}dx^{j};\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)\\
&=\int_{\tilde{\text{C}}_{\Gamma}}(d+d_{\bar{x}})\omega_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}dx^{j};\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)\\
&\quad\pm\int_{\partial\tilde{\text{C}}_{\Gamma}}\omega_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}dx^{j};\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg).\end{split}

Remark 9.4.4
.

In principle,
d
is the differential on
M\times\mathrm{C}_{\Gamma}
, hence it can be decomposed as
d=d_{x}+d_{1}+d_{2}
, where
d_{1}
denotes the part of the differential acting on the propagator and
d_{2}
the part acting on
\mathbb{B}
fields (and, more generally, on
\mathbb{A}
fields). We do not have a corresponding antiholomorphic differential on
M\times\mathrm{C}_{\Gamma}
since the propagators and the fields are all holomorphic. This is different with respect to the case considered in
[
CMW19
]
.

As in
[
CMW19
]
, we have
d\omega_{\Gamma}=d_{x}\omega_{\Gamma}
and in the boundary integral we have three classes of faces. The first two types of faces, where more than two bulk points collapse and where a subgraph
\Gamma
collapse at the boundary, can be proved as in
[
CMW19
]
. In particular, the former vanishes by our assumptions that the theory is anomaly-free (see Assumption
9.4.1
), while the second produces exactly the term
\frac{1}{2}\bigg[\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{\mathrm{pert}},\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{\mathbb{B}}_{\mathrm{pert}}\bigg]
by
[
CMW19
, Lemma 4.9]
. On the other hand, the third case, when two bulk vertices collapse, has some differences with respect to the analogous situation in
[
CMW19
]
due to the already mentioned further presence of red vertices. Here we distinguish four cases:

•

when a red vertex collapses with a black vertex, then these faces cancel out with

d_{x}\omega_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)
by the dCME (
4.2.12
);

•

when a black vertex collapses with a red vertex, then these faces cancel out with

d_{\bar{x}}\omega_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}dx^{j}\bigg)
by the dCME (
4.2.14
);

•

when two black vertices collapse, then these faces cancel out with
d_{x}\omega_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}dx^{j}\bigg)
by the dCME (
4.2.11
);

•

when two red vertices collapse, then these faces cancel out with
d_{\bar{x}}\omega_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)
by the dCME (
4.2.13
).

By
\omega_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\bigg)
or
\omega_{\Gamma}\bigg(\Big(\hat{R}_{k}\Big)_{j}dx^{j}\bigg)
, we mean the part of the subgraph
\Gamma^{\prime}
, which contains a red or black vertex.

This proves (
9.4.6
), thus
(\nabla_{\textup{G}})^{2}\equiv 0
.
∎

9.5.
Proof of the mdQME for the globalized split RW model

In this section, we are going to prove the mdQME for the globalized split RW model. The proof follows similarly as in
[
CMW19
]
. As before, we will refer to their work when the situation is identical and point out eventual differences.

Theorem 9.5.1

(mdQME for anomaly-free globalized split RW model)
.

Consider the full covariant perturbative state
\hat{\psi}_{\Sigma_{3},x}
as a quantization of the anomaly-free globalized split RW model. Then

(9.5.1)

\bigg(d_{M}-i\hbar\Delta_{\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma_{3},x}}+\frac{i}{\hbar}\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\partial\Sigma_{3}}\bigg)\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R}=0.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}
denote the set of Feynman graphs of the theory. Then, we can write the full covariant quantum state for the globalized split RW model as

(9.5.2)

\boldsymbol{\hat{\psi}}_{\Sigma_{3},x,R}=T_{\Sigma_{3}}\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}}\int_{\text{C}_{\Gamma}}\omega_{\Gamma}\Big(\hat{R}_{j}dx^{j};\hat{R}_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\Big),

where the combinatorial prefactor
\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\loops(\Gamma)}}{|\Aut(\Gamma)|}
is included in
\omega_{\Gamma}
(by
\loops
we denote the number of loops of a graph
\Gamma
) and we denote the configuration space
\text{C}_{\Gamma}(\Sigma_{3})
by
\text{C}_{\Gamma}
for simplicity. We note that
\omega_{\Gamma}
is a (
\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma_{3},x}
-dependent) differential form on
\text{C}_{\Gamma}\times M
. Again, following
[
CMW19
]
, we can apply Stokes’ theorem (
9.4.3
) and we get

(9.5.3)

d_{M}\int_{\text{C}_{\Gamma}}\omega_{\Gamma}\Big(\hat{R}_{j}dx^{j};\hat{R}_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\Big)=\int_{\text{C}_{\Gamma}}(d+d_{\bar{x}})\omega_{\Gamma}\Big(\hat{R}_{j}dx^{j};\hat{R}_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\Big)-\int_{\partial\text{C}_{\Gamma}}\omega_{\Gamma}\Big(\hat{R}_{j}dx^{j};\hat{R}_{\bar{j}}dx^{\bar{j}}\Big).

As mentioned in Remark
9.4.4
, the
d
inside the integral is the total differential on
\text{C}_{\Gamma}(\Sigma_{3})\times M
, and thus we can split it as

(9.5.4)

d=d_{x}+d_{1}+d_{2},

where
d_{1}
denotes the part of the differential acting on the propagators in
\omega_{\Gamma}
and
d_{2}
is the part acting on
\mathbb{B}
and
\mathbb{A}
fields.

With this setup, which is basically analogous to the one in
[
CMW19
]
, except for the presence of the red vertices and
d_{\bar{x}}
already extensively discussed, Eq. (
9.5.1
) is verified by proving three relations

•

a relation between the application of
d_{1}
and of
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}_{\Sigma_{3},x}}
to the quantum state,

•

a relation between the application of
d_{2}
and of
\Omega_{0}
to the quantum state,

•

a relation between the application of
d_{M}
and of the boundary contributions to the quantum state.

The proofs of these relations can be carried from
[
CMW19
]
over to the globalized split RW model without any problem. The only difference is when they prove that the contributions in
\partial\text{C}_{\Gamma}
consisting of diagrams with two bulk vertices collapsing vanish (which is needed for the third relation). In our case one should consider again three contributions: when two bulk black vertices collapse, when two bulk red vertices collapse, when a red vertex and a black one collapse. The vanishing of these terms follows from Eqs. (
4.2.11
), (
4.2.12
), (
4.2.13
), (
4.2.14
). The rest of the procedure is identical to
[
CMW19
]
.
∎

10.
Outlook and future direction

Our globalization construction leads to an interesting extension of some aspects in the program presented in
[
CLL20
]
for manifolds with boundary and cutting-gluing techniques. In particular, it would be of interest to understand some relations to the deformation quantization of Kähler manifolds in the guise of
[
RT99
]
, especially using the constructions of
[
CMW20
]
, and Berezin–Toeplitz quantization as presented in
[
Sch10
]
(possibly for the noncompact case).
It also leads to a more general globalization construction of an algebraic index theory formulation by using the BV formalism together with Fedosov’s globalization approach as presented in
[
GLL17
]
. Moreover, it might also be related to a case of twisted topological field theories, known as Chern–Simons–Rozansky–Witten TFTs, constructed by Kapustin and Saulina in
[
KS09
]
. In particular, they use the BRST formalism to produce interesting observables as Wilson loops and thus one might be able to combine it with ideas of
[
ABM13
,
Mos20
]
. Another direction would be the study of the RW invariants through our construction for hyperKähler manifolds. We guess that this would require studying observables of RW theory in the BV-BFV formulation, but the globalization procedure should tell something about these 3-manifold invariants. We hope that this might also be compatible with some generalizations of RW invariants in the non-hyperKähler case as discussed in
[
RS02
]
.

Appendix A
Topological quantum field theories

This appendix gives a brief introduction to perturbative and functorial constructions of topological (quantum) field theories, especially we recall Atiyah’s TQFT axioms.

A.1.
Brief introduction to perturbative quantum field theory

On a spacetime manifold
\Sigma
, consider a space of fields
19
19

19

The space of fields is usually given by sections of some vector bundle over
\Sigma
.

F_{\Sigma}
and an action functional
S_{\Sigma}
which is required to be
local
. This means that the action is the integral of a density-valued Lagrangian
\mathscr{L}
, called
Lagrangian density
, depending on the fields and on a finite number of higher derivatives. In particular,
S_{\Sigma}:F_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, with

(A.1.1)

S_{\Sigma}(\phi)=\int_{\Sigma}\mathscr{L}(\phi,\partial\phi,\dots),

where
\phi\in F_{\Sigma}
is a field. The set of data consisting of
(\Sigma,F_{\Sigma},S_{\Sigma})
defines a classical Lagrangian field theory.

During the years, physicists have developed several approaches to quantum field theory. Roughly, we can split them into perturbative and non-perturbative methods. Here, we focus on the former. Note that by perturbative, we mean semiclassical: in physics jargon perturbation theory is the idea of expanding through a formal power series around the coupling constant of the action. In the perturbative setting, the protagonist of the story is the
partition function

Z
, which encodes all the information about the quantum theory it portraits. In general, we can express it through a path integral as

(A.1.2)

Z_{\Sigma}=\int_{F_{\Sigma}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{\Sigma}(\phi)}\mathscr{D}[\phi],

where
\hbar
is the reduced Planck constant.

Remark A.1.1
.

In (
A.1.2
),
\mathscr{D}
denotes a formal measure on
F_{\Sigma}
. Depending on the space of fields
F_{\Sigma}
, this measure is often mathematically ill-defined. Nevertheless, one can define (
A.1.2
) by considering the methods of
perturbative expansion
around critical points of
S_{\Sigma}
in a formal power series in
\hbar
with coefficients given by
Feynman graphs
(see e.g.
[
FH65
,
Pol05
]
).

Let us make the above discussion more precise. Consider
\Sigma
to be a manifold with boundary
\partial\Sigma
and
B_{\partial\Sigma}
to be the space of boundary values of the fields on
M
. Since the boundary manifold is the boundary of
\Sigma
, we have a restriction map
F_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\pi}B_{\partial\Sigma}
. The partition function is thus a complex-valued function on
B_{\partial\Sigma}
which can be written as

(A.1.3)

Z_{\Sigma}(\phi_{\partial\Sigma};\hbar)=\int_{\{\phi\in F_{\Sigma}\mid\ \phi|_{\partial\Sigma}:=\phi_{\partial\Sigma}\}}e^{\frac{i}{\hbar}S_{\Sigma}(\phi)}\mathscr{D}[\phi],

where
\phi_{\partial\Sigma}
is a point in
B_{\partial\Sigma}
.

The manifold
\Sigma
may be complicated and, as a result, the computation of
Z_{\Sigma}
can become difficult. Therefore, it would be desirable to cut
\Sigma
into smaller and, hopefully, easier pieces, compute the partition function there and then glue them together to get the overall state. Suppose,
\Sigma
is closed and cut it in two disjoint manifolds
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
along a common boundary
\Sigma
, i.e.
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\sqcup_{\partial\Sigma}\Sigma_{2}
. If we paste them together, we expect the following condition to hold

(A.1.4)

Z_{\Sigma}=\int_{\phi_{\partial\Sigma}\in B_{\partial\Sigma}}Z_{\Sigma_{1}}(\phi_{\partial\Sigma})Z_{\Sigma_{2}}(\phi_{\partial\Sigma})\mathscr{D}[\phi_{\partial\Sigma}].

A.2.
Brief introduction to functorial quantum field theory

The functorial approach to QFT was developed by Segal in the context of conformal field theory
[
Seg88
]
and by Atiyah for TQFT
[
Ati88
]
. However, this description is general and it allows us to describe any QFT.

According to Atiyah’s axioms, an
n
-dimensional topological field quantum field theory consists of the following set of data:

(1)

A Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}(\Sigma)
, called the
space of states
, associated to a closed oriented
20
20

20

The orientation endows the manifolds with symbols
\{in,out\}
which denote
incoming
or
outgoing
orientation.

(n-1)
-manifold
\Sigma
,

(2)

A linear map of vector spaces
Z_{M}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{in}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{out}}
, called
partition function
, associated to an oriented
n
-cobordism
21
21

21

See Example
A.2.1
for a definition.

M
from
\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}
to
\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
(i.e. the boundary of
M
is assumed to be given as
\partial M=\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\sqcup\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
).

(3)

Orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms
\phi:\Sigma_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma_{2}
which act on
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
through unitary maps
\rho(\phi):\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}
, with
\rho
a representation.

(4)

Orientation-reversing identity diffeomorphisms
s_{\Sigma}:\Sigma\rightarrow\bar{\Sigma}
, where we denote by
\bar{\Sigma}
, the manifold with opposite orientation. These diffeomorphisms act by
\mathbb{C}
-anti-linear maps
\sigma_{\Sigma}\coloneqq\rho(s_{\Sigma}):\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\bar{\Sigma}}
.

This set of data is required to satisfy the following axioms:

(i)

(Multiplicativity) For two closed oriented
(n-1)
-manifolds
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
, the space of states is multiplicative, i.e.

(A.2.1)

\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime}}=\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}.

For two
n
-cobordisms
M:\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}\rightarrow\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}
and
M^{\prime}:\Sigma^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}}\rightarrow\Sigma^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}
, the partition function is multiplicative

(A.2.2)

Z_{M\sqcup M^{\prime}}=Z_{M}\otimes Z_{M^{\prime}}:\quad\mathcal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{in}}\otimes\mathcal{\Sigma^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{in}}\rightarrow\mathcal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{out}}\otimes\mathcal{\Sigma^{\prime}}_{\mathrm{out}}

(ii)

(Gluing) Let
M_{1}:\Sigma_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma_{2}
,
M_{2}:\bar{\Sigma}_{2}\rightarrow\Sigma_{3}
be two
n
-cobordisms, the glued cobordisms can be constructed by gluing along the common
\Sigma_{2}
-component as
M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma_{2}}M_{2}:\Sigma_{1}\rightarrow\Sigma_{3}
. The associated partition function is then obtained by composing the partition functions for
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
as linear maps:

(A.2.3)

Z_{M_{1}\cup_{\Sigma_{2}}M_{2}}=Z_{M_{2}}\circ Z_{M_{1}}:\quad\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{1}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{3}}.

(iii)

(Involutivity)
Z(\bar{\Sigma})=Z(\Sigma)^{\vee}
, where
Z(\Sigma)^{\vee}
is the dual vector space.

(iv)

\mathcal{H}_{\emptyset}=\mathbb{C}
and
Z_{\Sigma\times[0,1]}=\Id:\mathcal{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{\Sigma}
.

(v)

For
\phi:M\rightarrow M^{\prime}
a diffeomorphism, the following diagram commutes:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}_{\mathrm{in}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}_{\mathrm{out}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\phi\mid_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{in}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho(\phi\mid_{\Sigma_{\mathrm{out}}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{M^{\prime}}}

It follows that
Z_{M}
is invariant under diffeomorphisms of
M
relative to its boundary components.

(vi)

(Symmetry) The natural diffeomorphism
\Sigma\sqcup\Sigma^{\prime}\rightarrow\Sigma^{\prime}\sqcup\Sigma
is sent by
\rho
to the natural isomorphism
\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
.

(vii)

The partition function for the cylinder
\Sigma\times[0,1]
viewed as a cobordism
\Sigma\times\bar{\Sigma}\rightarrow\emptyset
composed with the anti-linear map
(\sigma_{\Sigma})^{-1}:\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}
yields the Hermitian inner product
\braket{-,-}:\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\times\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

Let
M
be a closed
n
-manifold, which can be regarded as a cobordism
\emptyset\rightarrow\emptyset
. The associated partition function
Z_{M}\in\mathbb{C}
is an invariant under orientation-preserving diffeomorphisms on
M
.
In general, for a mapping torus

\frac{\Sigma\times[0,1]}{\Sigma\times\{0\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi}}{{\sim}}\Sigma\times\{1\}}
with
\phi:\Sigma\rightarrow\Sigma
a gluing diffeomorphism, the axioms above imply that
Z=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}}\rho(\phi)
. In particular, for the product manifold
\Sigma\times S^{1}
formed by identifying the opposite ends of the cylinder:

(A.2.4)

Z_{\Sigma\times S^{1}}=\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}}(\Id)=\dim\mathcal{H}_{\Sigma}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

This implies that the space of states is finite-dimensional.

A.2.1.
Atiyah’s axioms for TQFTs

Atiyah’s axioms can be reformulated in the categorical language. We start with some prerequisites before arriving to the definition of a TQFT.

Let us consider a symmetric monoidal category
\mathbf{C}
: it is a category equipped with a bifunctor
\otimes:\mathbf{C}\times\mathbf{C}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
, called
monoidal product
which allows to, roughly speaking, “multiply” objects. This product is well-defined because it is associative up to natural isomorphisms (in jargon it satisfies the
pentagon equations

[
Mac71
]
). Moreover, a monoidal category is symmetric when for all the objects
A,B\in\mathbf{C}
there are natural isomorphisms

(A.2.5)

\beta_{A,B}:A\otimes B\rightarrow B\otimes A

compatible with the associativity of the monoidal structure (they satisfy the
hexagon equations

[
Mac71
]
).

Example A.2.1
.

We consider two examples, which we need for later:

(1)

The category
\mathbf{Vect}_{\mathbb{K}}
whose objects are
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces for some field
\mathbb{K}
and morphisms are
\mathbb{K}
-linear maps. It is monoidal with the usual tensor product as monoidal product (
\otimes:=\otimes_{\mathbb{K}}
) and with unit
\boldsymbol{1}:=\mathbb{K}
. Moreover, one can show that it is symmetric.

(2)

The category of oriented
cobordisms
,\mathbf{Cob}^{\text{or}}_{n}
. The objects are oriented closed
(n-1)
- dimensional manifolds and morphisms are diffeomorphisms classes of bordisms. In a more down to Earth language, this means that the morphisms are given by the bulk of an oriented compact
n
-dimensional manifold with boundary, whose boundary components are the objects. We can compose a morphism with another morphism simply by gluing along the common boundaries. It has a monoidal structure where the monoidal product is given by the disjoint union and the unit object is the empty set
\emptyset
viewed as an
(n-1)
-dimensional manifold. The objects are endowed with orientations labeled by symbols
\{\mathrm{in},\mathrm{out}\}
.

Atiyah’s axioms can be reformulated in a short way as:

Definition A.2.2

(Topological field theory)
.

Let
(\mathbf{C},\otimes)
be a symmetric monoidal category. An
n
-dimensional oriented closed topological field theory (TFT) is a symmetric monoidal functor

(A.2.6)

Z:\mathbf{Cob}^{\text{or}}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}.

Definition A.2.3

(Topological quantum field theory)
.

An
n
-dimensional oriented topological quantum field theory (TQFT) is a symmetric monoidal functor

(A.2.7)

Z:\mathbf{Cob}^{\text{or}}_{n}\rightarrow\mathbf{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}.

Remark A.2.4
.

Note that the target category contains also infinite-dimensional vector spaces. However, an analogue of Eq. (
A.2.4
), implies that the state spaces are finite-dimensional.

Remark A.2.5
.

As seen in Definition
A.2.3
, the category of smooth oriented cobordisms is usually used to describe a TQFT. However, cobordisms may possess other geometric structures such as conformal structure, spin structure, framing, boundaries, etc. Consequently, the associated field theory will be conformal QFT, spin or framed TQFT, etc. For example, for Yang-Mills theories and sigma models, the source category is the category of smooth Riemannian manifolds with a collar at the boundary.

Example A.2.6
.

As first example, let us consider a cobordism represented by some pair of pants with genus 1 (see Fig.
A.2.1
). The TQFT
F
assigns to each boundary component a Hilbert space, i.e.
Z(\partial_{k}\Sigma)=\mathcal{H}_{k}
for
k=1,2,3
. Since
Z
is a symmetric monoidal functor, we have
Z(\partial_{1}\Sigma\sqcup\partial_{2}\Sigma\sqcup\partial_{3}\Sigma)=\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{3}
. As said before, each cobordism comes with a certain orientation:
\partial_{1}\Sigma
as well as
\partial_{2}\Sigma
are incoming boundaries (which we denote in the figure by an incoming arrow), while
\partial_{3}\Sigma
is an outgoing boundary (which we denote in the figure by an outgoing arrow). Associated to
\partial_{1}\Sigma
and
\partial_{2}\Sigma
, we have an incoming Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\text{in}}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\vee}_{2}\cong\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
and an outgoing Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{\text{out}}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{3}
associated to
\partial_{3}\Sigma
. The state
\psi
corresponding to this
cobordism and the given TQFT is given as the value of the morphism represented by the genus 1 pair of pants above (i.e. the bounding manifold
M
) under
F
.

\uparrow
\uparrow
\uparrow
M

Figure A.2.1
.
Cobordism
M
represented by pair of pants of genus 1 with boundary components
\partial_{1}\Sigma
,
\partial_{2}\Sigma
,
\partial_{3}\Sigma
.

Example A.2.7
.

As already mentioned in Section
A.2
, a closed manifold
\Sigma
can be seen as a cobordism
\emptyset\rightarrow\emptyset
. We can cut it in two disjoint manifolds
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
along a common boundary
\partial\Sigma
, i.e.
\Sigma=\Sigma_{1}\sqcup_{\partial\Sigma}\Sigma_{2}
. Then we can assign an opposite orientation to
\partial_{1}\Sigma_{1}
with respect to the orientation of
\partial_{1}\Sigma_{2}
. The same can be done to
\partial_{2}\Sigma_{1}
with respect to the orientation of
\partial_{2}\Sigma_{2}
. The two manifolds with boundary
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
can be glued back together to recover the partition function of the closed manifold
\Sigma
, see Fig.
A.2.2
.

\Sigma_{1}

\Sigma_{2}

Figure A.2.2
.
Gluing of two manifolds
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
along a common boundary
\partial\Sigma
.

Remark A.2.8
.

It is important to highlight that the functorial approach to TQFT is not based on any perturbative framework, therefore, its nature is intrinsically non perturbative.

Successively, in
[
BD95
]
, Baez and Dolan suggested enhancing Atiyah’s notion of TQFT to a functor from the
(\infty,n)
-extension of the cobordism category. Their idea is to allow gluing as well as cutting with higher codimension data. Moreover, they conjectured these TQFTs to be completely classifiable: this conjecture is known as the
Cobordism hypothesis
. In
[
Lur09
]
, Lurie provided a complete classification result for fully extended TQFTs formulated in the language of
(\infty,n)
-categories, a generalization of the notion of a category.

Appendix B
Elements of formal geometry

In this appendix, we want to explain some of the important notions of formal geometry developed in
[
GK71
,
Bot11
]
which are used for the globalization procedure.

B.1.
Formal power series on vector spaces

Let
V
be a finite dimensional vector space. The polynomial algebra on
V
is given by

(B.1.1)

\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee})=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\infty}\Sym^{k}(V^{\vee}).

If we choose
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
to be a basis of
V
, with dual basis
\{y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\}
, then elements
f\in\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee})
are given by

(B.1.2)

f(y)=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}=1}^{\infty}f_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}y_{1}^{i_{1}}\dots y_{n}^{i_{n}}=\sum_{I}f_{I}y^{I},

with only finitely many non-vanishing
f_{I}
. We have denoted by
I
a multi-index and
y^{I}=y^{i_{1}}\dots y^{i_{n}}
,
y^{\emptyset}\coloneqq 1
.

This algebra can be completed to the algebra of formal power series
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(V^{\vee})
, with infinitely many nonzero coefficients
f_{I}
. Note that both algebras
\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee})
and
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(V^{\vee})
are commutative, with the multiplication of formal power series or polynomials, respectively, generated by
V^{\vee}
. One can specify derivations of these algebras by their value on these generators, therefore, the map

(B.1.3)

\displaystyle V\otimes\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\Der(\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee}))

(B.1.4)

\displaystyle v\otimes f

\displaystyle\mapsto\Big(V^{\vee}\ni\alpha\mapsto\alpha(v)\cdot f\Big)

is an isomorphism with inverse

(B.1.5)

\displaystyle\Der(\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee}))

\displaystyle\rightarrow V\otimes\Sym^{\bullet}(V^{\vee})

(B.1.6)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}e_{i}\otimes D(y^{i}).

In coordinates this corresponds to sending
e_{i}\mapsto\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}
.

B.2.
Formal exponential maps

Let
M
be a manifold and let
\varphi:U\rightarrow M
, with
U\subset TM
an open neighbourhood of the zero section. For
x\in M
,
y\in T_{x}M\cap U
, we can write
\varphi(x,y)=\varphi_{x}(y)
.

Definition B.2.1

(Generalized exponential map)
.

We call
\varphi
a
generalized exponential map
if for all
x\in M
we have that

(1)

\varphi_{x}(0)=x
,

(2)

d\varphi_{x}(0)=\Id_{T_{x}M}
.

In local coordinates

(B.2.1)

\varphi_{x}^{i}(y)=x^{i}+y^{i}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{x,jk}^{i}y^{j}y^{k}+\frac{1}{3!}\varphi_{x,jkl}^{i}y^{j}y^{k}y^{l}+\dots,

where the
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
are respectively the base and the fiber coordinates. Two generalized exponential maps are identified if their corresponding jets agree at all orders.

Definition B.2.2

(Formal exponential map)
.

A
formal exponential map
is an equivalence class of generalized exponential maps. A formal exponential map is completely specified by the sequence of functions
\Big(\varphi_{x,i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}^{i}\Big)_{k=0}^{\infty}
.

From now on, we will abuse notation and we will denote equivalence classes and their representatives by
\varphi
. One can produce a section
\sigma\in\Gamma(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M))
from a formal exponential map
\varphi
and a function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
, via
\sigma_{x}=\mathrm{T}\varphi_{x}^{*}f
, with
\mathrm{T}
the Taylor expansion in the fiber coordinates around
y=0
and the pullback defined by any representative of
\varphi
. We will denote this section by
\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}f
, and note that it is independent of the choice of the representative since it only depends on the jets.

B.3.
Grothendieck connection

Definition B.3.1

(Grothendieck connection)
.

On
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt} 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M)
we can define a flat connection
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
, satisfying the property that

(B.3.1)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}\sigma=0\iff\sigma=\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}f,

for some

. Namely,
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}=d+R
with

, a 1-form with values in derivations

. In local coordinates,

is defined as

and

(B.3.2)

R_{i}(x;y)\coloneqq-\bigg[\bigg(\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial y}\bigg)^{-1}\bigg]^{k}_{j}\frac{\partial\varphi^{j}}{\partial x^{i}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}=Y_{i}^{k}(x;y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}.

Hence, we have

(B.3.3)

R(x;y)=R_{i}(x;y)dx^{i}=Y_{i}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}dx^{i}.

The connection
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
is called the
Grothendieck connection
23
23

23

In the setting of field theory we have called this the
classical
Grothendieck connection in order to distinguish it from its quantum counterpart.
.

For
\sigma\in\Gamma(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M))
,
R(\sigma)
is expressed via the Taylor expansion (in the
y
coordinates) of

(B.3.4)

-d_{y}\sigma\circ(d_{y}\varphi)^{-1}\circ d_{x}\varphi:\Gamma(TM)\rightarrow\Gamma(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M)),

and therefore,
R
does not depend on the coordinate choice. For a vector field
\xi=\xi^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
, we have

(B.3.5)

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\xi}=\xi+\hat{\xi},

with

(B.3.6)

\hat{\xi}(x;y)=\iota_{\xi}R(x;y)=\xi^{i}(x)Y_{i}^{k}(x;y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}.

Remark B.3.2
.

The classical Grothendieck connection is flat (i.e.
D^{2}_{G}=0
). Moreover, the flatness condition translates into

(B.3.7)

d_{x}R+\frac{1}{2}[R,R]=0,

which is a
Maurer–Cartan
(MC) equation for
R
.

Remark B.3.3
.

It can be proved that its cohomology is concentrated in degree 0 and is given by

(B.3.8)

H^{0}_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}}\Big(\Gamma(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M))\Big)=\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\cong\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M).

B.4.
Formal vertical tensor fields

Let
E\rightarrow M
be any
tensorial bundle
24
24

24

A tensorial bundle is any bundle which is a tensor product or antisymmetric or symmetric product of the tangent or cotangent bundle, or a direct sum thereof.
, for example
E=\bigwedge^{k}TM
. Its sections are called
tensor fields of type E
.

Definition B.4.1

(Formal vertical bundle)
.

The associated
formal vertical bundle
to
E
is then
\hat{E}\coloneqq E\otimes\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M)
. Its sections are called
formal vertical tensors of type
E
.

Remark B.4.2
.

These bundles can be thought of as tensors of the same type on
TM
where the dependence on fiber directions is formal.

The formal exponential map defines an injective map

(B.4.1)

\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}:E\rightarrow\hat{E}

via the Taylor expansion of a tensor field pulled back
25
25

25

Note that
\varphi
is a local diffeomorphism and hence we can define the pullback of contravariant tensors as the pushforward of the inverse.
to
U
by
\varphi
.

Furthermore, we can let
R
act by formal derivatives and therefore, we get a Grothendieck connection
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}=d+R
on any formal vertical tensor bundle. Similarly, as before, we have:

•

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
is flat;

•

flat sections of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
are precisely the ones in the image of
\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}
;

•

the cohomology of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
is concentrated in degree 0 and given by the flat sections, i.e.
\hat{E}
-valued 0-forms.

B.5.
Changing the formal exponential map

We will denote by
\varphi
be a family of formal exponential maps depending on a parameter
t
belonging to an open interval
I
. One can then associate to this family a formal exponential map
\psi
for the manifold
M\times I
by

(B.5.1)

\psi(x,t,y,\tau)\coloneqq(\varphi_{x}(y),t+\tau),

with
\tau
the tangent variable to
t
. The corresponding connection
\tilde{R}
is defined as follows. Let
\tilde{\sigma}
be a section of
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}\Big(T^{\vee}(M\times I)\Big)
, by definition we have:

(B.5.2)

\tilde{R}(\tilde{\sigma})=-(d_{y}\tilde{\sigma},d_{\tau}\tilde{\sigma})\circ\begin{pmatrix}(d_{y}\varphi)^{-1}&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\circ\begin{pmatrix}d_{x}\varphi&\dot{\varphi}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Hence,
\tilde{R}=R+Cdt+T
, with
R
defined as in Section
B.3
, but with a
t
-dependence now, and
T=-dt\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}
.

The MC equation (
B.3.7
) can be reformulated for
\tilde{R}
observing that :

•

d_{x}T=d_{t}T=0
,

•

T
commutes with
R
and
C
.

The
(2,0)
-form component of the MC equation over
M\times I
yields again the MC equation for
R
, while the
(1,1)
- component reads

(B.5.3)

\dot{R}=d_{x}C+[R,C].

Remark B.5.1
.

Under a change of formal exponential map,
R
changes by a gauge transformation having as generator the section
C
of
\hat{\mathfrak{X}}(TM)\coloneqq TM\otimes\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M)
. Finally, if
\sigma
is a section in the image of
\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}
, a simple computation yields

(B.5.4)

\dot{\sigma}=-L_{C}\sigma.

One can think of it as the associated gauge transformation for sections.

B.6.
Extension to graded manifolds

The previous results can be generalized to the category of graded manifolds exploiting the algebraic reformulation of formal exponential maps developed in
[
LS17
]
.

More concretely, given a formal exponential map
\varphi
on a smooth manifold
M
, one can construct a map

(B.6.1)

\pbw:\Gamma(\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(TM))\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(M)

from sections of the completed symmetric algebra of the tangent bundle to the algebra of differential operators
\mathcal{D}
by defining

(B.6.2)

\pbw\Big(X_{1}\odot\dots\odot X_{n}\Big)(f)=\frac{d}{dt_{1}}\Bigg|_{t_{1}=0}\dots\frac{d}{dt_{n}}\Bigg|_{t_{n}=0}f\Big(\varphi(t_{1}X_{1}+\dots+t_{n}X_{n})\Big),

where we denote by
\odot
the symmetric product.
One can also define this map in the category of graded manifolds by choosing a torsion-free connection
\nabla
on the tangent bundle of a graded manifold
M
with Christoffel symbols
\Gamma^{k}_{ij}
. In particular, there still exists an element

R^{\nabla}\in\Omega^{1}\Big(M,TM\otimes\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt} 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M)\Big)
with the property that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}=d_{M}+R^{\nabla}
is a flat connection on
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt} 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M)
, i.e.

(B.6.3)

R^{\nabla}=-\delta+\Gamma+A^{\nabla}.

In local coordinates
\{x^{i}\}
on
M
and
\{y^{i}\}
on
TM
, we have

(B.6.4)

\begin{split}\delta&=dx^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}},\\
\Gamma&=-dx^{i}\Gamma^{k}_{ij}(x)y^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}},\\
A^{\nabla}&=dx^{i}\sum_{|J|\geq 2}A^{k}_{i,J}(x)y^{J}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}.\end{split}

We define
R_{i}\in\Gamma(M,\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt} 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M)\otimes TM)
and
Y_{i}^{k}\in\Gamma(M,\hbox{\hskip
