via

(B.6.5)

R^{\nabla}=R_{i}(x;y)dx^{i}=Y_{i}^{k}(x;y)dx^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}.

In particular note that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
extends to a differential on
\Omega^{\bullet}(M,\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M))
. T

The Taylor expansion of a function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
can be defined as
[
LS17
]

(B.6.6)

\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}f\coloneqq\sum_{I}\frac{1}{I!}y^{I}\pbw\Big(\underset{\leftarrow}{\partial_{x}^{I}}\Big)(f),

where

(B.6.7)

\Big(\underset{\leftarrow}{\partial_{x}^{I}}\Big)=\underbrace{\partial_{x_{1}}\odot\dots\odot\partial_{x_{1}}}_{i_{1}}\odot\dots\odot\underbrace{\partial_{x_{n}}\odot\dots\odot\partial_{x_{n}}}_{i_{n}}.

One can prove that (
B.6.6
) has still the same properties, i.e. the image of
\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}
consists precisely of the
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}
-closed sections of
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M)
.

We can describe how the exponential map varies under the choice of a connection mimicking the construction for the smooth case described in Section
B.5
. More concretely, assume we have a smooth family
\nabla^{t}
of connections on
TM
, then we can associate to that family a connection
\tilde{\nabla}
on
M\times I
. The associated
R^{\tilde{\nabla}}
can be split as in Section
B.5

(B.6.8)

R^{\tilde{\nabla}}=R^{\nabla^{t}}+C^{\nabla^{t}}dt+T,

where
C\in\Gamma(M,\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(T^{\vee}M))
. As previously,
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}^{2}=0
means

(B.6.9)

\dot{R}^{\nabla^{t}}=d_{M}R^{\nabla^{t}}+[C^{\nabla^{t}},R^{\nabla^{t}}],

and for any section
\sigma
in the image of
\mathrm{T}\varphi^{*}
we have

(B.6.10)

\dot{\sigma}=-L_{C^{\nabla^{t}}}\sigma.

Appendix C
Elements of derived geometry

In Section
2
, we have introduced the BV formalism as a way to deal with non-isolated critical points for the action of a gauge theory. In other words, this means that the critical locus of the action functional (i.e. the set of points such that
\delta S=0
) is singular. The BV formalism instructs us to resolve the singularities homologically by taking the
derived critical locus
of the action functional, which is a smooth object in the category of derived spaces: this is done by the Koszul resolution of the critical locus. More generally, this procedure can be understood globally in the setting of
derived algebraic geometry
(DAG)
[
Toë14
,
Pan+13
]
. However, for the present work, we do not require the whole DAG language. For us it is sufficient to work with a “tamed” version of DAG, namely the framework developed by Costello in
[
Cos11
,
Cos11a
]
to deal with formal mapping stacks which capture the geometry of derived critical loci in nonlinear sigma models.

C.1.
Category of derived manifolds

Here, we want to define the category of derived manifolds. Let us start with the objects.

Denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
the de Rham algebra of a manifold
M
, which, in other words, is a sheaf of commutative differentially graded algebras.

Definition C.1.1

(Derived manifold,
[
Cos11
]
)
.

A derived manifold (over
\mathbb{R}
) is a pair
(M,\mathcal{A})
, where
M
is a smooth manifold and
\mathcal{A}
is a sheaf of unital differentially graded
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
, satisfying the conditions

(1)

As a sheaf of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-algebras,
\mathcal{A}
is locally free and of finite rank.

(2)

There is a morphism
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
of sheaves of
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
-algebras and the kernel of this map is a sheaf of nilpotent ideals.

(3)

The topology of
M
has a basis such that the cohomology
\mathcal{A}(U)
is concentrated in nonpositive degrees for each basis set of
U
.

Example C.1.2
.

Trivially, any manifold
M
with
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
is a derived manifold.

Example C.1.3
.

Let
M
be a manifold, take
\mathcal{A}=\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
equipped with a de Rham differential. The pair
(M,\Omega^{\bullet}(M))
is derived manifold which we denote by
M_{\,\text{dR}}
.

Example C.1.4
.

Let
M
be a complex manifold, take
\mathcal{A}=\Omega^{0,\bullet}(M)
equipped with a Dolbeault differential
\bar{\partial}
. The pair
(M,\Omega^{0,\bullet}(M))
is complex derived manifold.

Definition C.1.5

(Morphisms of derived manifolds)
.

A morphism of derived manifolds
(M,\mathcal{A})\rightarrow(N,\mathcal{B})
is a smooth map
f:M\rightarrow N
together with a morphism
\phi:f^{-1}\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
of
f^{-1}\Omega^{\bullet}(N)
-algebras such that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}\mathcal{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{-1}\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}

commutes.

Notation C.1.6
.

We denote by
\mathbf{DMan}
the category with objects given by derived manifolds and morphisms given by the ones we have just defined.

The notion of morphisms between derived manifolds is further enriched by introducing
weak equivalences
between derived manifolds. For this purpose, we will use the nilpotent differential graded (dg) ideal
I
of
(M,\mathcal{A})
defined as the kernel of the map
\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
. Here, we have a filtration by powers of the nilpotent ideal. Let
\Grad\mathcal{A}
denote the associated graded algebra with degree
k
part
\Grad^{k}\mathcal{A}\coloneqq F^{k}\mathcal{A}/F^{k+1}\mathcal{A}
and the induced differential.

Definition C.1.7

(Weak equivalence)
.

A morphism
(f,\phi):(M,\mathcal{A})\rightarrow(N,\mathcal{B})
of derived manifolds is a
weak equivalence
if
f
is a differomoprhisms and the induced map

(C.1.1)

\Grad\phi:f^{-1}\Grad\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\Grad\mathcal{A}

is a quasi-isomorphism.

Having a filtration has also another aim: it should mirror the role of the tower of quotients of a local Artinian algebra in formal deformation theory. In that context, in many situations, it is useful to proceed by
Artinian induction
: let
(A,\mathfrak{m})
be a local Artinian algebra over
\mathbb{R}
, there is a tower

(C.1.2)

A=A/\mathfrak{m}^{n+1}\rightarrow A/\mathfrak{m}^{n}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow A/\mathfrak{m}\cong\mathbb{R}.

This tower is then used to prove some properties of
A
. Following these ideas, in fact, derived manifolds can be used to study derived deformation theory as Artinian algebras are used to study formal deformation theory. Now, let us define Artinian dg algebras and make these ideas more precise. We will be concise so we refer to
[
Cos11
,
CG16
]
for a more detailed exposition.

Definition C.1.8

(Artinian dg algebra)
.

An
Artinian dg algebra

R
over a field
\mathbb{K}
is a finite dimensional dg algebra over
\mathbb{K}
, concentrated in non positive degrees, with a unique nilpotent dg ideal
\mathfrak{m}
such that
R/\mathfrak{m}\cong\mathbb{K}
.

The relation between Artinian dg algebra and derived manifolds is explained by the following Proposition

Proposition C.1.9

(
[
GG14
]
)
.

There is a fully faithful embedding

(C.1.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Spec:\mathbf{dgArt}^{op}_{\mathbb{K}}&\rightarrow\mathbf{DMan},\\
R&\mapsto\Spec R\coloneqq(\pt,R).\end{split}

The importance of Artinian dg algebras comes from being a sort of “test object” in formal derived deformation theory.

Definition C.1.10

(Formal derived moduli problem,
[
Lur11
]
)
.

A formal derived moduli problem over
\mathbb{K}
is a functor

(C.1.4)

X:\mathbf{dgArt}_{\mathbb{K}}\rightarrow\mathbf{sSets},

where
\mathbf{sSets}
is the category of simplicial sets and
X
is such that
X(\mathbb{K})
is contractible and
X
preserves certain homotopy limits.

Remark C.1.11
.

Loosely speaking, Artinian dg algebras are points with nilpotent directions in derived manifolds. Hence, studying formal moduli problems corresponds to studying the formal neighbourhoods of such points.

Remark C.1.12
.

We can now combine Definition
C.1.10
and Definition
C.1.9
. We generalize the formal derived moduli problems by extending the functor
X
to a functor
\mathbf{DMan}^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{sSets}
. In this way, we can study formal moduli problems parametrized by a smooth manifold
M
(before they were parametrized by an Artinian algebra).

C.2.
Derived stacks

In this section, we are going to introduce briefly the
derived stacks
. These are the spaces studied in derived algebraic geometry.

Recall the functor of points approach in algebraic geometry: a scheme can be defined as a functor from the category of commutative
\mathbb{K}
-algebras, i.e.
\mathbf{CAlg}_{\mathbb{K}}
, to the category of sets. Motivated by the study of moduli problems, since the focus was to classify objects with their isomorphisms, the target category was extended to the category of groupoids (a small category whose morphisms are invertible). These new functors were called stacks. A further generalization is called
higher stacks
, where the interest is to classify objects up to a higher notion of equivalence rather than isomorphisms (e.g. quasi-isomorphisms). The target category in this case is extended to the category of simplicial sets. Finally derived stacks (or derived higher stacks) arrive when we enlarge the source category to
\mathbf{DCAlg}_{\mathbb{K}}
, i.e. the category of simplicial commutative
\mathbb{K}
-algebras. This category has a natural model category structure, which allows to do homotopy theory. Hence, derived stacks are defined as functors
\mathbf{DCAlg}_{\mathbb{K}}\rightarrow\mathbf{sSets}
which send equivalences in the source category to weak homotopy equivalences on the target and satisfy a
descent
condition
[
Toë06
]
.

The related definition in Costello’s approach
[
Cos11
,
Cos11a
]
is similar, the only difference is for the source category which is the category of derived manifolds
\mathbf{DMan}^{op}
.

Definition C.2.1

(Derived stack)
.

A
derived stack
or
derived space
is a functor:

(C.2.1)

X:\mathbf{DMan}^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{sSets}

such that:

•

X
takes weak equivalences of derived manifolds to weak equivalences of simplicial sets.

•

X
satisfies Čech descent.

The notion of Čech descent is outside the scope of the present work, we refer to
[
GG14
,
Ste17
]
for a definition.

In the following, we will study a particular type of derived stack with a geometric interpretation, i.e. the derived stack represented by
L_{\infty}
-spaces.

C.3.
L_{\infty}
-spaces

The heart of the philosophy of deformation theory consists of the following statement: “every formal derived moduli problem is represented by an
L_{\infty}
-algebra”. For the explicit statement see
[
Lur10
]
. We will see how this works in our setting, but before, we need some definitions.

Definition C.3.1

(Curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra over
A
)
.

Let
A
be a commutative differential graded algebra (cdga) with a nilpotent dg ideal
I
and
A^{\#}
be the underlying graded algebra, with zero differential. A
curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra over
A
is a finitely generated projective
A^{\#}
-module
V
together with a derivation of cohomological degree 1:

(C.3.1)

d:\hbox{\hskip Remark C.3.2
.

If we take the Taylor components of

\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-10.37502pt\hbox{$\Sym$}\hskip-10.37502pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\bullet}(V[1]^{\vee})
, is known as the
Chevalley–Eilenberg complex
of
V
, and denoted in the following by
C^{\bullet}(V)
.

Definition C.3.4

(Morphism of curved
L_{\infty}
-algebras)
.

A
morphism of curved
L_{\infty}
-algebras

\phi:V\rightarrow W
over
A
is a map
\pi^{*}:C^{\bullet}(W)\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(V)
of cdga’s over
A
which respects the filtration by the ideal
I
.

Definition C.3.5

(Maurer–Cartan element, Maurer–Cartan equation)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be the curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra and
\alpha
a degree 1 element of
\mathfrak{g}
. The
Maurer–Cartan element
associated to
\alpha
is

(C.3.4)

MC(\alpha)=\sum^{\infty}_{n=0}\frac{1}{n!}\ell_{n}(\alpha^{\otimes n}).

The
Maurer–Cartan equation
for
\alpha
is
MC(\alpha)=0
.

Remark C.3.6
.

To render (
C.3.4
) well-defined, since it involves an infinite sum, we will only consider Maurer–Cartan elements in
nilpotent

L_{\infty}
-algebras.

As we have preannounced before, formal moduli problems are described by
L_{\infty}
-algebras. Explicitly, let
\mathfrak{g}
be an
L_{\infty}
-algebra and
R
be an Artinian dg algebra over
\mathbb{R}
, with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
. Then, we can associate a formal derived moduli problem to
\mathfrak{g}
by sending
R
to the simplicial set

(C.3.5)

MC(\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{m})

of solution of the Maurer–Cartan equation of the nilpotent
L_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathfrak{m}
. More precisely, the Maurer–Cartan functor
MC_{\mathfrak{g}}
is a formal derived moduli problem (see
C.1.10
). Moreover, if we send an
L_{\infty}
-algebra to its associated Maurer–Cartan functor, we obtain an equivalence of categories
[
Lur10
]
.

However, we are interested in
L_{\infty}
-algebras parametrized by smooth manifolds and derived stacks, the global counterparts to the notion of formal moduli problems, which has a local nature.

Definition C.3.7

(curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra over
\Omega^{\bullet}(X)
,
L_{\infty}
-space)
.

Let X be a smooth manifold.

(1)

A
curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra over
\Omega^{\bullet}(X)
consists of a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded topological
26
26

26

By topological, we mean that the fibers are topological vector spaces and the transition maps are continuous.
vector bundle
\pi:V\rightarrow X
and the structure of an
L_{\infty}
-algebra on its sheaf of smooth sections, denoted by
\mathfrak{g}
, where the base algebra is over
\Omega^{\bullet}(X)
with nilpotent ideal
I=\Omega^{\geq 1}(X)
.

(2)

An
L_{\infty}
-space is a pair
(X,\mathfrak{g})
, where
\mathfrak{g}
is a curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra over
\Omega^{\bullet}(X)
.

Now, we will explain how every
L_{\infty}
-space defines a derived stack. It works in the same manner as
L_{\infty}
-algebras determine formal moduli problems. Let
B\mathfrak{g}\coloneqq(X,\mathfrak{g})
denote an
L_{\infty}
-space. For a smooth map
f:Y\rightarrow X
, a curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra is formed over
\Omega^{\bullet}(Y)
as

(C.3.6)

f^{*}\mathfrak{g}\coloneqq f^{-1}\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{f^{-1}\Omega^{\bullet}(X)}\Omega^{\bullet}(Y).

Definition C.3.8

(
B\mathfrak{g}
functor of points,
[
Cos11a
,
GG14
]
)
.

Let
\Delta^{n}
be the standard
n
-simplex in
\mathbb{R}^{n}.
For
B\mathfrak{g}
an
L_{\infty}
-space, its
functor of points
is the functor

(C.3.7)

MC_{B\mathfrak{g}}:\mathbf{DMan}^{op}\rightarrow\mathbf{sSets},

which sends a derived manifold
\mathcal{M}=(M,\mathcal{A})
to the simplicial set
MC_{B\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{M})
whose
n
-simplices are given by pairs
(f,\alpha)
where
f:M\rightarrow X
is a smooth map and
\alpha
a solution of the Maurer–Cartan equation in the nilpotent curved
L_{\infty}
-algebra
f^{*}\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{\Omega^{\bullet}(M)}I_{\mathcal{M}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\Omega^{\bullet}(\Delta^{n})
, where
I_{\mathcal{M}}
is the nilpotent ideal. In other words,

(C.3.8)

MC_{B\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{M})=\bigsqcup_{f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,X)}MC(f^{*}\mathfrak{g}\otimes_{\Omega^{\bullet}(M)}I_{\mathcal{M}}).

Theorem C.3.9

(
[
GG14
]
)
.

For any
L_{\infty}
-space, its functor of points is a derived stack.

Remark C.3.10
.

Since in (
C.3.8
) we are tensoring with the nilpotent ideal
I_{\mathcal{M}}
instead of the whole algebra, the
L_{\infty}
-algebra is nilpotent. This reflects the idea that the deformation functor
MC_{B\mathfrak{g}}
should deform only the nilpotent directions of a derived manifold.

When
X
is a complex manifold, the following theorem provides us with all the needed properties for its
L_{\infty}
-space.

Theorem C.3.11

(
[
Cos11a
]
)
.

Let
X
be a complex manifold and let
\Omega^{\#}(X)
be the de Rham complex endowed with the zero differential. There exists an
L_{\infty}
-space
X_{\bar{\partial}}=(X,\mathfrak{g}_{X})
with the following properties:

(1)

\mathfrak{g}_{X}=\Omega^{\#}(X)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)}T^{1,0}X[-1]
as a
\Omega^{\#}(X)
-module.

(2)

C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}_{X})\cong\Omega^{\bullet}(X)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)}\Jet^{\mathrm{hol}}_{X}
as a
\Omega^{\bullet}(X)
-algebra.

(3)

The jet prolongation map

(C.3.9)

\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)\hookrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(X)\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(X)}\Jet^{\mathrm{hol}}_{X}\cong C^{\bullet}(\mathfrak{g}_{X})

is a quasi-isomorphism of complexes of sheaves.

C.4.
Derived mapping spaces

For an
L_{\infty}
-space, we can think of its functor of points
MC_{B\mathfrak{g}}
as the derived stack of maps into
B\mathfrak{g}
. With this idea in mind, in this section, we will see that if
(M,\mathcal{A})
is a derived manifold, a subset of the space of maps
(M,\mathcal{A})\rightarrow(X,\mathfrak{g})
is itself represented by an
L_{\infty}
-space.
Hence, let us define a new
simplicial presheaf
(see
[
GG14
]
) on the site of
\mathbf{DMan}
given by

(C.4.1)

\displaystyle MC^{\mathcal{M}}_{B\mathfrak{g}}:\mathbf{DMan}^{op}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbf{sSets},

(C.4.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}

\displaystyle\mapsto MC_{B\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{N}).

This functor is again a derived stack since
MC_{B\mathfrak{g}}
is a derived stack. In particular, this is the derived stack of maps from
\mathcal{M}
to
B\mathfrak{g}
. In perturbation theory, when we have a space of fields given by the space of maps
\mathcal{M}\rightarrow B\mathfrak{g}
, we perturb around a subset of these maps, usually the constant maps. Here we do the same. We will consider the sub-simplicial presheaf

(C.4.3)

\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-9.32707pt\hbox{$MC$}\hskip-9.32707pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\re 451.29065pt}^{\mathcal{M}}_{B\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{N})\subset MC^{\mathcal{M}}_{B\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{N})

of Maurer–Cartan solutions in which the underlying smooth map
M\rightarrow X
is constant. More precisely, for an auxiliary derived manifold
(\mathcal{N},\mathcal{B})
, consider

(C.4.4)

\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-9.32707pt\hbox{$MC$}\hskip-9.32707pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\re 451.29065pt}^{\mathcal{M}}_{B\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{N})\subset MC(\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{M})

which consists of Maurer–Cartan elements
(f,\alpha)
such that the underlying smooth map

factors through the projection onto

. Costello showed that this space is itself an
L_{\infty}
-space under certain conditions. This is useful for us since we would like
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt}^{\mathcal{M}}_{B\mathfrak{g}}
to represent the space of fields of a classical field theory.

Proposition C.4.1

(
[
Cos11
,
Cos11a
]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}=(M,\mathcal{A})
be a derived manifold with nilpotent sheaf of ideals
I
and with the property that, if
\mathcal{A}
is filtered by the powers of the nilpotent ideal, then the
cohomology
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{G}}^{i}\mathcal{A}
f ( M) for
i\geq 1
is concentrated in degrees
\geq 1
.

Let
(X,\mathfrak{g})
be an
L_{\infty}
-space such that the cohomology of the sheaf of
L_{\infty}
-algebras
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{red}}\coloneqq\mathfrak{g}/\Omega^{\geq 1}(X)
.

Then, the restricted Maurer–Cartan functor
\hbox{\hskip 441.65944pt\hskip-8.06105pt\hbox{$\text{MC}$}\hskip-8.06105pt\hskip-441.65944pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{52312104}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0 441.65944pt}^{\mathcal{M}}_{B\mathfrak{g}}
is weakly equivalent to the functor of points for the
L_{\infty}
-space
(X,\mathfrak{g}\otimes\mathcal{A}(M))
.

Notation C.4.2
.

From now on, we will write
\hbox{\hskip 451.29065pt\hskip-12.89815pt\hbox{$\Maps$}\hskip-12.89815pt\hskip-451.29065pt\raisebox{17.83344pt}{\hbox{$\hbox{\raisebox{-3.34055pt}{\resizebox{53617400}{141084}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\mathchoice{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-853.45891pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{10.00012pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14236pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.4pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.98pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}{\raisebox{-157.14285pt}{\resizebox{468892385}{36044800}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\displaystyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\textstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.7pt}{$\scriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{0}{0}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.5pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\kern-0.6pt\bigwedge\kern-0.6pt$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}}}}$}}\hskip-451.29065pt\hskip 451.29065pt}(\mathcal{M},B\mathfrak{g})
for the
L_{\infty}
-space
(X,\mathcal{A}(M)\otimes\mathfrak{g})
.

C.5.
Shifted symplectic structures

In
[
Sch93
]
, Schwarz gave a definition of shifted symplectic structure on a dg manifold. Since in dg manifolds, all spaces of tensors are cochain complexes, the space of
i
-forms
\Omega^{i}(M)
on a dg manifold is a cochain complex with a differential called the
internal
differential. Moreover, we have also the de Rham differential
d_{\mathrm{dR}}:\Omega^{i}(M)\rightarrow\Omega^{i+1}(M)
. According to Schwarz a symplectic form is an element of the complex of 2-forms that is both, closed with respect to the de Rham differential and the internal differential. This element is also required to be non-degenerate. If the internal degree
27
27

27

The internal degree is also called “ghost” degree.
of the form is
k
, it is said to be
k
-shifted. The physical relevant degrees are
-1
for the space of bulk fields (BV formalism) and 0 for the boundary phase space (BFV formalism).

In
[
Pan+13
]
, Pantev et al. gave a definition of shifted symplectic structure in the context of derived geometry using the language of derived Artin stacks. Here, a closed
2
-form is a cocycle in the truncated de Rham complex

(C.5.1)

\Omega^{2}_{cl}(X)\coloneqq\bigg(\bigoplus_{k\geq 2}\Omega^{k}(X)[-k+2],d_{\mathrm{dR}}\bigg)

shifted in such a way that
\Omega^{2}(X)
is in degree zero.

Remark C.5.1
.

Note that a closed
2
-form is given by a sequence of forms
(\omega_{2},\dots,\omega_{k},\dots)
, with
\omega_{k}
a form of degree
k
and finitely many nonzero forms, such that
d_{\mathrm{dR}}\omega_{k}=\pm d_{\mathrm{int}}\omega_{k+1}
for
k\geq 2
, where
d_{\mathrm{int}}
is the internal differential. Therefore, to say that a 2-form is closed we need to specify more data than just a 2-form. Hence, being closed is a datum, it is not anymore a property like in the smooth case.

In particular, a 2-form is symplectic when it is non-degenerate in a suitable sense (see
[
Pan+13
]
).

In
[
CG16
]
, it is shown that a symplectic form of degree
k
in the sense of Schwarz is the same as a degree
k-2
non-degenerate invariant symmetric pairing on
\mathfrak{g}
. Moreover, we have the following lemma that closes the circles between all these apparently different notions of a symplectic form.

Lemma C.5.2

(
[
CG16
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be a finite dimensional
L_{\infty}
-algebra. A
k
-shifted symplectic structure in the sense of
[
Pan+13
]
on
B\mathfrak{g}
is the same as a degree
k-2
non-degenerate invariant symmetric pairing on
\mathfrak{g}
.

Hence, a
k
-shifted symplectic structure on an
L_{\infty}
-space
(X,\mathfrak{g})
can be defined to be such a pairing on
\mathfrak{g}
.

Example C.5.3
.

Consider a complex manifold
X
of dimension
2n
, by endowing
X
with a holomorphic symplectic form (a non-degenerate 2-form on
T^{1,0}X
which is closed under
d_{X}=\partial+\bar{\partial}
, with
\partial
the holomorphic differential and
\bar{\partial}
the antiholomorphic differential), the
L_{\infty}
-space
X_{\bar{\partial}}
associated to
X
becomes 0-shifted symplectic.

\mathbb{B}
-representation
